# **Springer Texts in Business and Economics** 

Springer Texts in Business and Economics (STBE) delivers high-quality instructional content for undergraduates and graduates in all areas of Business/Management Science and Economics. The series is comprised of selfcontained books with a broad and comprehensive coverage that are suitable for class as well as for individual self-study. All texts are authored by established experts in their fields and offer a solid methodological background, often accompanied by problems and exercises.

**Part I Linear Algebra** 

Economics as a scientific discipline studies how individuals, groups, and organizations use scarce resources to achieve desired goals. This can refer to the production of products and services, the optimal distribution of goods, and other concepts. In doing so, observed phenomena are often quantified. 

Linear algebra is a useful mathematical tool that can be used to display such a large amount of data in a systematic way, but also to obtain new results, either by using different operations among data or by developing models that connect the measured data in a meaningful way and give new insights into the problem. Thus, in this part of the book, the basic elements of linear algebra necessary for economic analysis are presented, with a special emphasis on the development of mathematical tools that enable us to solve systems of linear equations. Such systems play a key role in setting up and solving many important microeconomic and macroeconomic models. 

**Matrices and Types of Matrices** 

**==> picture [32 x 32] intentionally omitted <==**

**1** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

The study of economic phenomena very often involves observation of a large amount of data that needs to be presented in a systematic manner and then used in a meaningful way to assess the situation and draw conclusions. Without the development of a suitable mathematical tool, it is often difficult to clearly present the situation and perform a meaningful analysis, even for small size problems. For example, a company that wants to monitor daily sales of its products through a retail network in several cities and use the data obtained in order to conduct various analyses can hardly do so without a systematic and transparent display of the data. Therefore, in this chapter we introduce the term matrix as well as basic types of matrices. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Matrix · Types of matrices · Identity matrix · Null-matrix · Symmetric matrix · Antisymmetric matrix · Main diagonal · Trace · Matrix equality 

The modern economic landscape is defined by an explosion of data. This surge, driven by digital technologies, e-commerce, sensor networks, and the digitalization of global economic activity, has fundamentally changed how we understand, model, and predict economic behavior. The sheer volume and velocity of this data require powerful and precise tools to organize and present data in a systematic and transparent manner, so that it can be used meaningfully to assess a situation, perform analysis and draw conclusions. Without a suitable mathematical tool, it is difficult to do so, even for small size problems. Linear algebra, the mathematical study of vectors, vector spaces, linear transformations, and, at its very foundation, matrices, provides the necessary framework to organize, analyze, and leverage this huge amount of data in economics. Data sets with millions of observations and hundreds

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

After we have introduced the concepts of a matrix, matrix equality, and the basic types of matrices, in this chapter we introduce basic operations with matrices. Four basic binary operations are defined in the set of real numbers: addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division. Therefore, we want to introduce their equivalents to the set of matrices, with similar properties as the corresponding binary operations in the set of real numbers. Thus, we introduce the operations of addition, subtraction, multiplication by a scalar, scalar product, and matrix multiplication in the set of matrices. Instead of the operation of division, we introduce the concepts of inverse and multiplication with inverse. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Matrix addition · Scalar multiplication · Matrix subtraction · Scalar product · Matrix multiplication · Inverse of a matrix · Singular matrix · Non-singular matrix

## **2.6 Inverse of a Matrix** 

In the set of real numbers, besides operations of addition, subtraction, and multiplication, we also have the operation of division. The operation of division is not defined in the set of matrices, but the basic idea of the division can be covered by the concept of an inverse and multiplication by an inverse. 

Let us first consider the operation of division in the set of real numbers. It is defined for all real numbers , where the divisor must not be 0. The division can also be seen as a multiplication, where is multiplied by the 1 inverse of the number , denoted Here, the inverse of a real number , denoted 1, is the real number with the property: 

**==> picture [67 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

A number with such a property exists for every real number 0, and moreover, it is uniquely determined. 

In a similar way, we introduce the notion of the inverse and multiplication by an inverse into the space of matrices. 

**Definition 2.13** Given matrix , if there exists a matrix such that 

**==> picture [4 x 3] intentionally omitted <==**

where is the identity matrix, then we say that is an **inverse** of . Moreover, in this case is said to be **invertible** . 

Note that an inverse can be defined for square matrices only. If and are not square matrices, the products and do not have to be defined, and since in general the commutativity property does not hold, even in the case when the both products do exist, they do not have to be equal. The question arises: do all square matrices have an inverse, or only some of them? If we look at the set of real numbers, number 0 is the only number without the inverse. Consider the null-matrix For any matrix we have 

**==> picture [3 x 3] intentionally omitted <==**

so the null-matrix certainly does not have an inverse. However, is it the only square matrix that does not have an inverse, or can we find some more? 1 0 **Example 2.19** Does the matrix have an inverse? 1 0 

**Solution** The matrix has an inverse

In various problems, it is often very important to know whether matrix is non-singular, and if so, to find its inverse. For example, many important problems in economics are described by a system of linear equations , where one needs to find the matrix of unknowns . Often, in such systems the number of equations is equal to the number of unknowns, i.e., , that is is a square matrix. If the matrix is also non-singular, the solution can be determined relatively easily by multiplying the entire matrix equation from the left by the inverse of the matrix : 

**==> picture [4 x 7] intentionally omitted <==**

Now we have 

**==> picture [67 x 30] intentionally omitted <==**

which gives the exact formula for the solution to the considered system, i.e., 

**==> picture [10 x 6] intentionally omitted <==**

Since inverse is unique, this also means that in the case of a non-singular matrix , the solution to the system is also unique. Here, we still haven’t tackled the problem of how to determine the inverse of the matrix . 

From all of the above, we see that there is a need for a simple criterion by which it would be possible to tell whether a square matrix is non-singular, and if so, to find its inverse. The notion of determinant, which we will deal with in the next chapter, will help us to do just that.

## **Determinants** 

**3** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

In the previous chapters, we pointed out the importance of having a clear criterion by which we can determine whether a matrix is non-singular, and if so, the existence of a method by which we can calculate its inverse. The term determinant plays a key role in this. There are different approaches to the definition of the determinant, but here we show the approach where the determinant is defined by induction. In doing so, we introduce the concepts of minor, cofactor and Laplace expansion. Also, we state the Sarrus rule for calculating determinants of the order 3. After giving its geometrical interpretation, we state the Binet-Cauchy theorem and apply it to derive a criterion on how to use the determinant to check if a matrix is non-singular. We also give the formula for the inverse of a matrix by using the adjoint, which is the transpose of the corresponding matrix of cofactors. Finally, we present Cramer’s rule for solving Cramer’s system, that is a system AX=B in which the number of equations is equal to the number of unknowns and matrix A is non-singular, as well as give two economic applications of Cramer’s rule—determining the equilibrium price in a market with multiple goods, the IS-LM model and the Keynesian model of income determination. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Determinant · Minor · Cofactor · Laplace expansion · Sarrus rule · Binet-Cauchy theorem · Matrix of cofactors · Adjoint · Cramer’s system · Cramer’s rule · Equilibrium price · IS-LM model · Keynesian model of income determination

## **3.1 Definition and Properties of the Determinant** 

The determinant is a real number that is assigned to each square matrix. If a matrix is not square, the determinant is not defined. 

There are different approaches to the definition of the determinant, but here we will show an approach where the determinant is defined by induction. Therefore, as a base of induction, we first define the term determinant for square matrices of the order 2.

## **Theorem 3.14 (Cramer’s Rule)** _Cramer’s system_ 

_has a unique solution and it is given by the formula_ 

**==> picture [180 x 21] intentionally omitted <==**

Note that this rule, unlike the relation (3.6), allows us to calculate the value of any single unknown in a simple way, without calculating the values of the remaining unknowns. In order to do so, it is necessary to find only two determinants: that of the matrix and of the matrix Namely, many important problems in economics are described by Cramer’s systems, and sometimes we are interested in only the value of one or few unknowns and not in the solution to the entire system. An additional advantage of this rule is that it is not necessary to determine the inverse 1, the calculation of which can be very demanding for problems of large dimensions.

**The IS-LM Model** The IS-LM model, also called Hicks-Hansen model, is a macroeconomic tool that illustrates the relationship between interest rates and real output in the goods and services market (IS curve) and the money market (LM curve). ## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

So far we have seen how to find the solution to a system of linear equations AX=B in the case of a Cramer’s system. In this chapter, we consider a general system of m linear equations with n unknowns and we want to find an answer to each of the following three important questions: 1. When does a system of linear equations have a solution? (existence of solutions) 2. If the solution exists, is it unique? 3. How to find a solution? In order to do so, we start by introducing the concepts of the linear combination of vectors, as well as the linear dependence and independence of a set of vectors. Since it is very difficult to verify if a set of vectors is linearly dependent or independent by using the definition itself, it is necessary to develop a mathematical apparatus that can help determine the required relationship among vectors in a simpler way. Therefore, we introduce the term rank and operations of elementary transformations that enable us to convert any matrix into a matrix for which the rank is obvious, without changing its rank. Now that we have the necessary tools, we can present the main results on systems of linear equations, that is find answers to the three questions from the beginning. We start by stating and proofing the important Kronecker-Capelli theorem, which gives the answer to the question about the existence of a solution. We proceed by presenting the Gauss-Jordan elimination method, which enable us to solve any system of linear equations, and we discuss the number of solutions. This method can also be used to find the inverse of a non-singular matrix, which gives an alternative method to finding the inverse. Finally, we present one of the most important applications of the systems of linear equations to economics, which is input-output analysis. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

System of linear equations · Coefficient matrix · Vector of unknowns · Vector of constants · Linear combination of vectors · Trivial linear combination ·

**Definition 4.3** The **trivial linear combination** of vectors 1 2 is a linear combination in which all coefficients 1 2 are equal to 0, i.e., 

0 for all 1 . 

A **non-trivial linear combination** of vectors 1 2 is a linear combination in which at least one of the coefficients 1 2 is not zero, i.e., there is an index 1 such that 0 

We have already seen that the existence of a solution to the system (4.3) depends on whether the vector can be represented as a linear combination of the vectors 1 2 Therefore, we introduce the concepts of linear dependence and independence. Here, the term linear dependence of a set of vectors is used to describe the situation where one vector from that set can be represented as a linear combination of the remaining ones, while the term linear independence is used to describe the case when this is not possible. 

**Definition 4.4** A set of vectors 1 2 is said to be **linearly dependent** if there exist scalars 1 2 of which at least one is not 0 such that 1 1 2 2 **0** (4.6) 

If the equality (4.6) holds only for 1 2 0, we say that the set of vectors 1 2 is **linearly independent** . 

Note that the relationship 

**==> picture [141 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

is always true. Therefore, a set of vectors is linearly independent if there is one and only one way to represent the null-vector **0** as a linear combination of vectors 1 2 , that is as the trivial linear combination. If there is also a non-trivial linear combination, the set of vectors 1 2 is linearly dependent. In other words, a set of vectors is linearly independent if the system (4.6) has a unique 

4 Systems of Linear Equations

solution (the trivial solution), and it is linearly dependent if there is at least one non-trivial solution in addition to the trivial one.

However, since the linear dependence and independence of vectors is as difficult to verify as it is to solve the corresponding system of linear equations, it is necessary to develop a mathematical apparatus that can help us determine the required relationship among vectors in a simpler way. Therefore, we introduce the term rank.

The question arises: is it possible to “simplify” a matrix, i.e., to transform it into another matrix that will have the same rank as the initial matrix, but for which it will be easy to read the rank? 

## **4.3 Elementary Transformations** 

In the previous chapter, we saw that multiplying a column of a matrix by a number different from 0 did not change the rank (Examples 4.6 and 4.7). It turns out that this property also holds in general. Furthermore, since the linear dependence or independence of vectors does not depend on their enumeration, it is easy to see that by interchanging any two columns (rows), the rank will not change. Finally, it can be shown that adding an arbitrary column (row) of a matrix to any another column (row) of that matrix does not change the rank. Using these operations we can convert 

4 Systems of Linear Equations 

96 

any matrix into a matrix for which the rank is obvious, without changing its rank. These operations are called **elementary transformations** . Thus, the elementary column (row) transformations of a matrix are: 

1. Interchanging any two columns (rows) of a matrix 

   - . 

2. Multiplying each element of a column (row) of a matrix by any scalar different from 0. 

3. Adding to each element of the -th column (row) corresponding element of the -th column (row) of a matrix . 

**Definition 4.8** If matrix can be transformed into matrix by applying a finite sequence of elementary transformations, then we say that and are **equivalent** and we write 

Since elementary transformations do not change the rank, it is obvious that equivalent matrices have the same rank. Our goal is to use elementary transformations to transform an arbitrary matrix into an equivalent matrix for which the rank is obvious. As we have already seen from the Example 4.6, the rank is obvious if a matrix consists of columns of the identity matrix, as illustrated by Fig. 4.1. 

The matrix obviously has rank . Therefore, the easiest way to find the rank of an arbitrary matrix is to transform it into a form illustrated by Fig. 4.1 by performing a sequence of elementary transformations. Here, both column and row elementary transformations are allowed.

## **4.5 Gauss-Jordan Elimination Method** 

The basic idea of finding the rank is to transform the matrix into an equivalent matrix for which the rank is obvious. In a similar way, the basic idea of the Gauss-Jordan elimination method is to convert the system of linear equations into an equivalent system for which the solution is obvious. If the solution is unique, this means that we can convert the initial system into an equivalent system of the form 1 1 (4.15) 2 2 

for which the solution is obvious. Note that the augmented matrix associated with the system (4.15) is equal to the matrix 

**==> picture [67 x 55] intentionally omitted <==**

that is, the coefficient matrix of the system (4.15) is equal to the identity matrix. 

The question is: which transformations can be used to convert the system into an equivalent one, that is to the one which has the same solution? Obviously, interchanging the order of two equations does not change the solution to the system. Also, if we multiply an equation within the system by a scalar which is not zero, the solution remains the same. And finally, if we add one equation to another, the solution does not change. Since any system of linear equations can be represented by the augmented matrix, this means that we can perform elementary transformations 

4 Systems of Linear Equations 

106 

**Fig. 4.3** Equivalent system 

**==> picture [157 x 100] intentionally omitted <==**

over the rows of the augmented matrix. Such transformations won’t affect the solution. The goal of these transformations is to convert the augmented matrix into an equivalent one that resembles the identity matrix as much as possible in the part that refers to the coefficient matrix (Fig. 4.3), that is in the part to the left of the vertical line. Unlike the procedure for finding the rank, here the key elements must have the value 1, and in all positions above and below the key element it is necessary to get the value 0 

When we start the procedure, we still don’t know if the solution exists, and if it does, whether it is unique or not. Therefore, we may not be able to achieve the identity matrix in the part of the augmented matrix that refers to the coefficient matrix. The idea is to convert the system into an equivalent one which contains as many columns of the identity matrix as possible. The maximum number of such columns will obviously be equal to the rank of the coefficient matrix . Once we have such a system, it is easy to determine whether the solution exists, as well as to read the solution, if there is one. Namely, if the rank of the coefficient matrix of thus obtained equivalent system is equal to the rank of the corresponding augmented matrix of that system, the solution exists. Otherwise, there is no solution. Figuratively speaking, if on Fig. 4.3 we get one more ‘stair’ after the vertical line, there is no solution. If the number of stairs remains the same (i.e., the horizontal line under the ‘stairs’ goes straight after the vertical line), the system is consistent. 

If the solution exists, i.e., if , we find it by reconstructing the corresponding system of linear equations from the augmented matrix of the equivalent system. Each row of the augmented matrix corresponds to one equation, while each column of the coefficient matrix corresponds to one unknown. 

Note that here we did not use elementary transformations over the columns. Namely, whenever we interchange two columns of the coefficient matrix, in each equation we interchange the coefficients next to the corresponding unknowns. The solution to such system is not the same as the solution to the initial system. Although it is possible to reconstruct the solution from the system obtained by interchanging columns, we will avoid such transformations. 

To summarize, the Gauss-Jordan elimination method converts the initial system of linear equations into an equivalent one which has as many columns of the identity matrix as possible. It does so by performing elementary transformations over the rows. Therefore, the following elementary transformations over the augmented matrix are allowed: 

4.5 Gauss-Jordan Elimination Method 

107 

1. Interchange any two rows (interchanging the position of two equations) 

2. Multiply each element of a row by any scalar 0 (multiply an equation by a scalar 0) 

3. Add to each element of the -th row the corresponding element of the -th row (adding one equation to another). 

As by convention we do not allow elementary transformations over the columns, it may not be possible to convert the system into an equivalent one for which the key elements are located exactly on the main diagonal.

Once we fix the value of these scalars, that is, the value of the variables 1 , the values of the variables 1 2 are uniquely determined. Therefore, we say that the variables 1 are **free** variables, and the number of such variables is reffered to as the number of **degrees of freedom** . Note that the number of free variables is equal to , where is the number of unknowns, and is the rank of the coefficient matrix. If the number of unknowns is equal to the rank, i.e., , the system obviously has a unique solution. Otherwise, it has a general solution with degrees of freedom, that is, unknowns can take any value and their choice uniquely determines the value of the remaining unknowns. This gives an answer to the question of how many solutions a system has.

## **4.7 Application in Economics: Input-Output Analysis** 

One of the most important applications of the systems of linear equations to economics is input-output analysis. It was proposed by the Russian-American mathematician Wassily Leontief, who won the Nobel Prize in 1973 for this contribution to economics. 

Input-output analysis is the model of national economy which describes the interrelationship among the parts which comprise it through the flow of goods and services among them. It assumes that the economy of a country is divided into sectors, where each sector produces one type of output, and each individual sector is related to all others, in the sense that the inputs of a particular sector are the outputs of that and the remaining sectors. Here, by the term “input” we mean everything that goes into production, while by the term “output” we mean everything that comes out of the production process. It should be noted that here the term “input” does not take into account labor nor raw materials that are not produced by other sectors. Each individual sector uses a specific set of inputs in order to produce only one type of output with a given technology. Part of the production of that output is used for 

4.7 Application in Economics: Input-Output Analysis 

121 

consumption (which we call **final demand/consumption** ), while part goes back to the production process as an input of production of the other sectors, as well as its own sector ( **intermediate output** ). 

The assumptions of the model are as follows: 

1. The production function of each sector in the model exhibits constant returns to scale (i.e., if we increase or decrease all inputs used for production of a certain sector by the same percentage, the output will also increase or decrease by that very same percentage). 

2. The technology remains the same, i.e. the ratio of inputs used for a production of a given sector is fixed. 

The problems this model tries to solve are as follows: 

- How much output is available for the final demand, given the total output level? 

- How much total output is required to satisfy a given level of final demand? 

- What changes need to be made to the total output if the final demand changes by a given amount? 

We introduce the following notation. Let denote the total output of the - th sector, let denote the final demand of the -th sector, and let denote the intermediate output, that is, the number of units of output of the -th sector used for the production of units of the -th sector, 1 This data is usually presented in a table of the form

As you can see from the diagram, the total output of the first sector is divided among between intermediate output (i.e., the amount 11 12 1 that other sectors use as inputs) and the final demand 1[which] is placed on market for consumption. Here, 11[is][the][amount][of][the][production][of] the first sector being sent back to the first sector as the input of production, 12[is] the amount of the production of the first sector being sent to the second sector as the input of production, etc. 

The total output of each sector is spent on the final demand and on what other sectors use as inputs. Therefore, we have

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

In the previous chapters we have already used the term vector, by which we meant a _n_ -component row matrix or column matrix, and we also spoke about spaces. At the same time, the interrelationship of vectors in the sense of linear dependence, i.e., independence, was crucial in checking the existence of the solution to a system of linear equations, as well as in determining its solutions. Therefore, for the sake of completeness, in this chapter we consider the concept of vectors and vector spaces, as well as normed spaces. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Euclidean vector space · Norm of a vector · Distance between vectors

## **5.2 The Norm of a Vector** 

Intuitively, the length of a vector **x** 1 2 is equal to the distance of the point 1 2 from the origin. However, how to measure this distance? We already know from the experience that the shortest (aerial) distance between two places is not equal to the actual distance that we have to travel by car, given that the road network most often does not follow the shortest air distance. This example suggests that we can measure distance in several ways. In a similar way, when we talk about vectors, we can measure their distance in several ways, depending on what we want to measure. However, we don’t use the term length, but the term norm of a vector. In this chapter we define three different norms, that is, three different ways of how to measure the vector distance. 

**Definition 5.7** The **Euclidean norm** of the vector **x** denoted **x** 2[,][is][equal][to][the number]

**Applications of Linear Algebra in Economics**

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

One of the common questions students ask when studying mathematics is: Why do I need this? Where can I apply this? The same is true for students of economics. This is why in this chapter we list some applications of linear algebra and its seemingly abstract concepts in economics. We have already explored some key applications to economics, like determining market equilibrium and inputoutput analysis. In this chapter we shortly review the important applications we have seen so far, as well as outline some more applications, demonstrating how the seemingly abstract concepts of vectors, matrices, and determinants provide concrete insights into real-world economic problems. We provide some applications of linear algebra to microeconomics and macroeconomics, linear programming, game theory, econometrics and regression analysis, as well as dynamic economic models. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Applications of linear algebra in economics · Linear programming · Game theory · Econometrics and regression analysis · Dynamic economic models 

As you know, economics is a science of scarcity and choice. It seeks to understand how individuals, businesses, and governments make decisions about allocating limited resources to satisfy unlimited wants and desires. In order to do that, modern economics is deeply quantitative, relying heavily on mathematical tools to model, analyze, and predict economic phenomena. Here, linear algebra stands out as one of its particularly useful tools since it provides a powerful apparatus for modeling, analyzing, and solving complex economic problems in various areas of economics, including microeconomics, macroeconomics, econometrics, and optimization. We have already explored some key applications to economics. In this chapter, we 

> © The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026 Z. Lukaˇc, _Economic Analysis Through Mathematics_ , Springer Texts in Business and Economics, https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-08812-3_6 

153 

6 Applications of Linear Algebra in Economics 

154 

will shortly review the important applications we have seen so far, as well as state some more applications, demonstrating how the seemingly abstract concepts of vectors, matrices, and determinants provide concrete insights into real-world economic problems. 

## **6.1 Applications in Microeconomics and Macroeconomics** 

One of the best known applications of linear algebra in economics is input-output analysis, as we have seen in Sect. 4.7. It is a macroeconomic analysis technique developed by Wassily Leontief that examines the interdependencies between different sectors or industries within an economy. Although it is often considered macroeconomics, the foundations of input-output analysis lie in microeconomic principles of firm behavior and industry interactions. In essence, it shows how the output of one industry becomes an input for another, illustrating the flow of goods and services throughout the economic system. It uses matrices to model the interdependencies between different sectors of an economy. Thus, the tools of linear algebra enable us to analyze the impact of changes in one sector on the entire economy, calculate total production required to meet final demand, and study trade relationships among different sectors, as well as between regions or businesses. Moreover, it allows us to estimate the economy-wide effects of various events, such as new investments, policy changes, etc., as well as to understand the intricate supply chains within an economy and the reliance of industries on one another. Furthermore, it pinpoints sectors that have a significant impact on the rest of the economy due to their strong interconnections and provides insights for economic planning by estimating the production levels needed in different sectors to meet specific targets. Thus, it explores the fundamental structure of an economy and the relationships among its different parts, as well as analyzes the impact of imports and exports on domestic industries. Therefore, input-output analysis provides a comprehensive framework for understanding the complex web of economic interactions and for quantifying the interconnectedness of different industries within an economy. As we have seen in Sect. 4.7, input-output analysis relies heavily on linear algebra, particularly matrix operations, to model and solve these interdependencies. 

Many macroeconomic models, such as simplified versions of the IS-LM model or models involving equilibrium conditions, are expressed as systems of linear equations. Linear algebra techniques like matrix inversion and Cramer’s rule can be used to find the equilibrium values of key macroeconomic variables (e.g., output, interest rates, price levels in simplified settings). We have seen two such applications in Sect. 3.5.1. Here, linear algebra helps in finding equilibrium conditions and analyzing the effects of policy changes. Also, governments and international organizations use macroeconomic models based on linear algebra for economic planning and policy analysis. These models can help simulate the potential effects of different policy interventions (e.g., changes in government spending, taxes, or monetary policy) on key macroeconomic variables. Another important application 

6.1 Applications in Microeconomics and Macroeconomics 

155 

deals with dynamic macroeconomic models which analyze how economies evolve over time. These models often involve systems of linear difference or differential equations. Linear algebra, particularly the concepts of eigenvalues and eigenvectors, is used to analyze the stability of equilibrium points in these dynamic systems and to determine the long-run behavior of economic variables (e.g., growth rates). Here, an eigenvalue of a square matrix is a real number with the property that there exists some vector **v 0** such that **v v** , with **v** being its corresponding eigenvector. For example, in analyzing economic growth models or models of business cycles, eigenvalues can indicate whether the economy converges to a steady state or experiences instability. Note that this book does not cover eigenvalues and eigenvectors. 

Another important application that we have seen so far is the problem of determining market equilibrium, that is, the point in which supply of each good equals its demand. At this point, there is no tendency for the price to change because both buyers and sellers are satisfied with the current price and quantity. While simple supply and demand curves can be analyzed graphically, in markets with multiple goods, linear algebra becomes indispensable. Here, the equilibrium prices and quantities are determined by equating the supply to the demand of each good. If supply and demand are linear functions, then this problem results in a system of linear equations in which the number of variables and the number of equations are equal to the number of goods. As we have seen, linear algebra provides methods to solve these systems efficiently, especially when dealing with a large number of variables. If the resulting system of linear equations is a Cramer’s system, it is easy to find the equilibrium price and the corresponding equilibrium quantity of any given good by applying the Cramer’s rule. We have seen one such application in the Sect. 3.5.1. 

Linear algebra is very important in microeconomics, since many economic models involve systems of linear equations. Here, vectors and matrices are used to represent preferences and utility functions. In consumer theory, utility functions describe a consumer’s preferences over different bundles of goods. For multiple goods, these functions can be represented using vectors (representing the quantities of each good) and matrices (to capture the relationships and trade-offs between goods, especially in more complex utility structures). Furthermore, a consumer’s budget constraint, which limits their spending, can be expressed as a linear equation or inequality. With multiple goods, this becomes a vector equation where the prices of goods are elements of a price vector and the quantities consumed are elements of a quantity vector. The budget constraint can then be represented as the scalar product of these two vectors being less than or equal to the consumer’s income. 

Regarding the equilibrium systems, we can also analyze how equilibrium prices and quantities change in response to changes in exogenous variables (like taxes or input prices), which is part of comparative statics. Furthermore, linear algebra can also be used within the context of welfare economics, since concepts like Pareto efficiency and the analysis of social welfare functions can sometimes be expressed and analyzed using linear algebra, particularly when dealing with multiple individuals and goods. In this wide range of microeconomic problems, 

Applications of Linear Algebra in Economics 

156 

6 

linear algebra provides a powerful and elegant framework for representing, solving, and analyzing problems. Its ability to handle systems of equations and vector relationships makes it a very important tool for understanding complex economic interactions and building sophisticated models. As microeconomic theory becomes more advanced and incorporates more variables and complexities, the importance of linear algebra continues to grow. 

Linear algebra is also particularly useful in optimization problems, like cost minimization and profit maximization problems involving multiple variables, with or without linear constraints. Such problems can be elegantly formulated and solved using matrix algebra and related techniques. The first-order conditions (which result in a system of equations whose solutions are candidates for extrema) can involve systems of linear equations, while the second-order conditions (which verify if a given candidate is indeed an extremum as well as if it is a maximum or a minimum) require the use of determinants, as we will demonstrate in the Chap. 18 regarding the optimization of functions of several variables. One example of such application is the derivation of the demand function for a given good. Here, the demand function is obtained by maximizing the consumer’s utility function (which represents the consumer’s preferences) subject to her budget constraint. It’s dual problem consist of determining the minimum expenditure needed to achieve some fixed level of the utility function, that is, the problem of determining the minimum income needed to achieve certain level of standard. The exact form of these two problems can be found in Chap. 19. 

## **6.2 Linear Programming** 

Many economic problems involve optimization—maximizing utility, minimizing costs, or maximizing profits—subject to various constraints (e.g., budget, resource availability, production capacity). When the objective function and all constraints are linear, the problem can be solved using **linear programming** , which is another important application of linear algebra. 

A typical linear programming problem seeks to maximize or minimize a linear objective function: 

**==> picture [158 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

subject to a set of linear inequality or equality constraints 

**==> picture [140 x 27] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [50 x 8] intentionally omitted <==**

6.2 Linear Programming 

157 

and non-negativity constraints 

**==> picture [112 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

In matrix notation, this becomes: 

**==> picture [132 x 11] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [82 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [24 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

where **c** is the vector of coefficients for the objective function, **x** is the vector of decision variables, is the matrix of constraint coefficients, and **b** is the vector of constraint limits. 

While solving linear programming problems often involves algorithms like the simplex method (which iteratively moves between vertices of the feasible region), the entire framework—from defining the problem to understanding its solution space—is built upon the principles of linear algebra, including concepts like feasible regions (polyhedra), extreme points, and duality. 

Linear programming has numerous applications in economics. Some typical applications include problems within the areas of production planning, transportation and logistics, supply chain management, energy management, financial planning, agriculture, marketing, as well as economic modeling and analysis. We will shortly describe each of them. 

Production planning cope with problems of determining optimal product mix, resource allocation, and production scheduling. Here, the optimal product mix deals with determining the most profitable combination of goods to produce given limited resources like labor, raw materials, and machine capacity (for instance, a factory has to decide how many units of different products to manufacture to maximize revenue while adhering to production constraints, etc.). Resource allocation deals with deciding how to allocate limited resources across different production processes or departments to maximize overall output or minimize costs. Production scheduling considers optimizing production schedules to meet demand while minimizing inventory holding costs and production costs, etc. 

Transportation and logistics problems deal with finding the most cost-effective routes for transporting goods from multiple sources to various destinations, considering factors like transportation costs, delivery times, and vehicle capacities. The main concern of supply chain management is to optimize the entire network of activities involved in creating and delivering a product or service to the end customer in the most efficient and effective way possible. Thus, it optimizes the flow of goods across the supply chain, including inventory management, warehousing, and distribution. 

Energy management deals with determining the optimal mix of energy sources to meet demand at the lowest cost while considering environmental and other regulations. 

Applications of Linear Algebra in Economics 

158 

6 

Linear programming also has numerous applications in financial planning, such as portfolio optimization (constructing investment portfolios that maximize returns for a given level of risk or minimize risk for a desired level of return, considering various asset classes and investment constraints) and capital budgeting (i.e., deciding which long-term investments or projects to undertake to maximize the company’s value, subject to budget limitations and project dependencies, etc.). It is also being used for financial modeling and forecasting, that is, for developing models to forecast financial variables and assess the impact of different economic scenarios. 

Some of the typical applications of linear programming in agriculture include farm management (like optimizing crop planting decisions, livestock production, and resource utilization of, e.g., land, water, fertilizers etc.) to maximize profits, as well as diet planning (i.e., formulating balanced diets at the minimum cost while meeting specific nutritional requirements). 

The application of linear programming to marketing include problems such as media planning (i.e., allocating advertising budgets across different media channels to maximize reach and effectiveness) and sales territory optimization, which includes designing sales territories to balance workload and maximize sales coverage. 

Another particularly important application is project scheduling. Project scheduling is a crucial part of project management, since every project, whether it’s building a house, a factory, or developing a new piece of software, or planning an event, is a complex web of interconnected tasks. A delay in one small component can create a ripple effect, pushing back deadlines and inflating budgets. The fundamental challenge of project scheduling is to understand the intricate dependencies between tasks to effectively allocate resources and identify potential bottlenecks. It is an indispensable tool for conducting complex projects for which a detailed, organized plan is needed to help plan the tasks, workforce, and resources, as well as to meet deadlines and deliver projects successfully. How do we do this? By using linear algebra! Project’s tasks, durations, and dependencies are represented using vectors and matrices, which enable us to move from a qualitative picture to a quantitative model. The solution to this model is something called a critical path— the sequence of tasks that cannot be delayed without delaying the entire project. Also, for each task, it calculates its slack, that is, the exact amount of time a task can be delayed without affecting the project’s final deadline. This is invaluable for resource allocation, as it tells a manager which tasks have flexibility and which do not. It also allows for the sensitivity analysis, that is, it provides answers to what-if questions: What if a task is delayed? What if a dependency is added? etc. 

Linear programming is outside of the scope of this book. However, as we can see from the list of applications, it is a very dynamic field which provides a structured and quantitative framework for economists and businesses to make optimal decisions in the face of limited resources and competing objectives. It helps find the best possible solution to complex problems, as well as the efficient allocation and utilization of scarce resources. By optimizing processes and resource allocation, it can lead to lower costs and higher profits. It provides a data-driven approach to decision-making and can help in developing effective long-term plans. 

6.3 Game Theory 

159 

## **6.3 Game Theory** 

Linear algebra is also used in game theory. Game theory is the study of strategic decision-making among rational agents. It has wide applications in economics, from understanding oligopoly behavior to bargaining and auction design. Linear algebra provides the tools to represent and analyze these strategic interactions. Here, the strategies of players can be represented as vectors (especially mixed strategies where a player randomizes over pure strategies), and the payoffs can be represented using matrices. For certain types of games (e.g., zero-sum games), finding Nash equilibria can involve solving systems of linear equations or inequalities. Linear algebra provides the tools to analyze the strategy spaces and payoff structures to identify these equilibrium points. 

As an illustration of application of linear algebra in game theory, let us consider a two-player, zero-sum game, where one player’s gain is another’s loss. This game can be represented by a payoff matrix : 

**==> picture [80 x 55] intentionally omitted <==**

where is the number of strategies for Player 1 and is the number of strategies for Player 2. If Player 1 chooses row and Player 2 chooses column , then is the payoff to Player 1 (and to Player 2). 

When players choose their strategies randomly, they are playing mixed strategies. Let **p** be a column vector of probabilities for Player 1’s strategies, and **q** be a column vector of probabilities for Player 2’s strategies: 

**==> picture [141 x 126] intentionally omitted <==**

The expected payoff for Player 1 is **p q** A key concept in game theory is the Nash Equilibrium, a set of strategies where no player can improve their payoff by unilaterally changing their strategy, given the other players’ strategies. For zero-sum games, the solution often involves finding optimal mixed strategies that minimize 

6 Applications of Linear Algebra in Economics 

160 

the maximum possible loss (minimax strategy). This can involve solving linear programming problems, which are fundamentally based on linear inequalities and linear objective functions. 

For more complex games, especially those with many players or dynamic interactions, concepts like eigenvalues and eigenvectors can be used to analyze the stability of certain strategies or the long-run behavior of the game. For instance, in evolutionary game theory, the dominant strategies in a population can be identified through the eigenvalues of a matrix representing the interaction dynamics. 

## **6.4 Econometrics and Regression Analysis** 

Linear algebra also finds its importance within the field of econometrics and statistical analysis. Linear regression, a fundamental tool for estimating relationships between economic variables, is entirely based on linear algebra. The estimation of coefficients, calculation of standard errors, and hypothesis testing all rely on matrix operations. Furthermore, concepts like linear independence, span, and orthogonality are essential for understanding the properties of estimators and the validity of statistical models. 

Econometrics is the application of statistical methods to economic data. Its primary goal is to give empirical content to economic theory and to test hypotheses. Linear algebra forms the foundation of one of the most widely used econometric techniques, which is ordinary least squares (OLS) regression. OLS regression is used to estimate the linear relationship between a dependent variable ( ) and one or more independent variables ( 1 2 ). 

Consider a multiple linear regression model:

Concepts like matrix rank, determinants, and eigenvalues are crucial for understanding the properties of the OLS estimator, such as its existence, uniqueness, and efficiency. 

Applications of Linear Algebra in Economics 

162 

6 

## **6.5 Dynamic Economic Models: State-Space Representation** 

Many economic phenomena are dynamic, evolving over time. Linear algebra provides a powerful framework for modeling these dynamics, particularly through linear difference equations and state-space models. These models are used in macroeconomics to analyze business cycles, monetary policy, and economic growth. 

A simple linear first-order difference equation can describe the evolution of an economic variable over time: 

**==> picture [51 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

For a system with multiple variables, we can have a system of linear difference equations 

**==> picture [69 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

where **x** is a vector of state variables at time , is a transition matrix, and **b** is a vector of constant terms or exogenous shocks. 

The long-run behavior and stability of such systems are determined by the eigenvalues of the matrix . If all eigenvalues have an absolute value less than 1, the system is stable and converges to a steady state. Eigenvectors can describe the directions of convergence or divergence. 

More generally, state-space representation is a powerful tool in control theory and econometrics for modeling dynamic systems. It involves defining a set of state variables that completely describe the system’s current state and then describing how these states evolve over time and how they relate to observable outputs. 

The applications discussed in this chapter represent just a fraction of the ways linear algebra permeates economic analysis. From foundational models like Leontief’s input-output framework that reveal inter-industry dependencies, through linear programming, to the statistical methods of econometrics and the dynamic models that forecast future trends, linear algebra provides the essential mathematical language. 

Its ability to handle multiple variables simultaneously, represent complex systems concisely, and provide systematic methods for solving problems makes it an indispensable tool for economists. Whether developing theoretical models, analyzing empirical data, or formulating policy recommendations, a solid understanding of linear algebra empowers economists to tackle the intricate challenges of the modern economy with rigor and precision. As economic models become increasingly complex and data-intensive, the importance of linear algebra will only continue to grow. 

**Part II** 

**Differential Calculus of Functions of One Real Variable** 

Functions play an important role not just in mathematics, but in many fields of science. They describe how variables are related to one another and enable the deeper understanding of that relationship. In economics, we are particularly interested in real functions, i.e., functions that describe relationships among real numbers. As important as it is to model the phenomenon, it is as equally important to understand the effect of change in one economic variable on the others. For example, it is important to know how a change in price will affect the demand, or how a change in money supply will affect the interest rate, or how a change in tax policy will affect investments, etc. Differential calculus is especially useful in doing this, since it provides insight into how a small change in one variable affects a change in another. It also enables us to detect in an easier manner if a function is increasing or decreasing over some interval, or if it is convex or concave. 

We will pay special attention to the development of techniques for finding local extrema, which is of particular interest in economics as a science that deals with the optimal use of resources. For example, it is important to determine the price level for which the maximum profit is achieved, the production level for which the minimum average costs are achieved, etc. In addition, we will also deal with the concept of elasticity, which shows how a percentage change in one economic variable affects another, which is very important in the theory of consumer behavior. 

We start with the basic terms related to functions. 

**Real Functions of One Real Variable**

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

Functions play an important role not just in mathematics, but in many fields of science. They describe how variables are related to one another and enable a deeper understanding of that relationship. In economics, we are particularly interested in real functions, i.e., functions that describe relationships among real numbers. As important as it is to model the phenomenon, it is as equally important to understand the effect that change in one economic variable has on the others. In this chapter we introduce the basic terms related to functions.

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

After a brief overview of the basic terms related to functions, as well as an overview of some common real functions of one real variable, in this chapter we introduce the concept of a limit. Limits deal with the question of what happens to the functional value when the independent variable moves closer and closer to some value . This term is one of the fundamental terms in differential calculus and plays a key role in the definition of continuity and differentiability of a function. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Limit · Properties of limits · Asymptotes

The concept of a limit is defined as follows. 

**Definition 8.1** Let be a function defined at all points of an interval except possibly at the point We say that the real number is the **limit** of a function at the point if for every real number 0 there exists a real number 0 such that for all real , 0 implies that If is the limit of the function at the point , we write

According to the definition, function has a limit at the point if for any arbitrarily small interval of the width around point , i.e., interval 1 , we can find the interval 2 around the point of width so that the function maps every point from the interval 2[,] except perhaps the point , into the interval 1[In][order][for][this][property][to][hold,] the problem is not the large values of the width , but the small ones. Note that the interval 1 consists of all points whose distance from is less than , while the interval 2 consists of all points whose distance from is less than We say that 1 is the **-neighborhood** of the point , while interval 2 is the - **neighborhood** of the point . Note that the interval 2[is][defined][by][the inequality] , while the interval 1[is][defined][by][the inequality] If we look at Fig. 8.1 , then informally speaking we can explain this term in the following way. A function has the limit at the point if for every arbitrarily narrow “strip” around the point of width we can find a sufficiently narrow “strip” around the point of width such that the function maps all points from the intersection of that strip with the -axis, except for the point , into some point of the strip of width around . In other words, for every , , whose distance from the point is less than , the distance of its image from the point is less than In the definition of the limit we have already said that it is not the value of the function at the point that is important, but its value in the vicinity of the point . ## **8.3 Asymptotes** 

We have already informally introduced the concept of an asymptote in the context of a rational function 

when we spoke about its horizontal and vertical asymptotes. 

However, in the general case, besides a horizontal and vertical asymptote, there may also be an oblique asymptote. Now that we know the definition of a limit, we can give a formal definition of an asymptote of the function defined on a subset of the set of real numbers 

**Definition 8.7** An **asymptote** of a curve is a line such that the distance between the curve and the line approaches zero as one or both of the and coordinates tend to infinity.

**Continuous Functions**

**==> picture [32 x 32] intentionally omitted <==**

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

The term “continuous” describes the property of a function which maps “close” points of the domain into “close” points of the codomain. If a function is continuous, the small changes in input variables result in small changes in output variables. Speaking informally, a real function of one real variable is continuous if we can draw it in one stroke, without lifting a pen from paper. A prerequisite for the definition of the concept of differentiability is that a function is continuous. The same assumption is assumed in many economic applications. The lack of this property has important economic consequences, as we illustrate by presenting some applications of continuity to problems in economics. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Continuous function · Properties of continuous functions · Application of continuity to problems in economics 

## **9.1 Definition and Properties** 

**Definition 9.1** Let and let be a real function defined on the set . We say that the function is **continuous** at point if for every (arbitrarily small) real number 0 we can find a real number 0 such that

Informally speaking, in a similar way to limits, a function is continuous at some point if for every arbitrarily narrow “strip” around the point of width , we can find a sufficiently narrow “strip” around the point of width such that function maps all points from the intersection of that strip and the -axis, including the point , into some point of the strip of width around Note that the difference between the definition of the term limit and the term continuous is that the term limit does not consider what happens at the point (moreover, a function does not even need to be defined at the point ), while with continuity it is very important what happens at the point . On the other hand, again informally speaking, we can say that a function has a limit at some point if at that point it can be “repaired” by one point so that it becomes continuous. 

Let us now check if a function is continuous by using the definition.

## **9.2 Some Application of Continuity to Problems in Economics** 

Here we show two examples that illustrate that continuity and the lack of continuity might have some very important economic consequences. 

## **9.2.1 A Salary with a Bonus Payment** 

Let a monthly salary of a salesperson consist of three parts: 

1. Fixed basic amount of $3 500 

2. 5% commission 

3. A bonus of $2 000 if the sales of that salesperson within a given month is greater or equal to $30 000 

Let denote the salesperson’s total monthly sales, and let denote her monthly salary. Then can be expressed as the following function : 

**==> picture [121 x 22] intentionally omitted <==**

This function is clearly discontinuous at the point 30 000, as illustrated in Fig. 9.3. We have 

**==> picture [225 x 16] intentionally omitted <==**

The discontinuity of this function at the point 30 000 has interesting economic consequences. Consider three salespersons, and let their cumulative sales till the last day of the month, excluding the last day, be as follows: 

- $34 000 for salesperson 1 

- $29 000 for salesperson 2 

- $18 000 for salesperson 3 

**Fig. 9.3** Monthly salary as a function of sales 

**==> picture [181 x 128] intentionally omitted <==**

9 Continuous Functions 

206 

What will be their incentive at work on the last day of the month? As each employee receives a 5% commission regardless of sales, this bonus represents the biggest incentive. Given that employee 1[has][already][earned][a][bonus,][and employee] 3[is][unlikely][to][make][a][sale][in][the][amount][of][$12] 000 within one day, employee 2[will][show][the][greatest][incentive.][Namely,][if][on][the][last][day][of][the][month][s][he] manages to make a sale in the amount of $1 000, in addition to the basic amount and the usual 5% commission, she will also receive a bonus and thus the difference in salary in the amount of $2 000 This is a direct consequence of a discontinuity of the monthly income function at the point 30 000 

## **9.2.2 Income Support Program** 

The care for the unemployed and designing measures that increase their incentive to work are important part of any good social program. Consider an example of one such program where the individuals who are unemployed receive a fixed monthly payment, but the payment stops as soon as a person finds any part-time job, regardless of the number of hours worked. Let $1 500 be a fixed monthly payment, and assume that a person has found a part-time job that pays $30 per hour. The monthly income of this person, , can be represented as a function of the number of hours worked, : 

**==> picture [63 x 22] intentionally omitted <==**

Since 

lim0 0 1 500 0 , 

this function is obviously discontinuous at the point 0, as illustrated in Fig. 9.4. This discontinuity has significant economic consequences. Namely, as soon as this person works for even one hour per month, her monthly income drops from $1 500 to $30, thus penalizing part-time work. If one adds the costs of transportation to work, as well as other costs arising from employment (such as the cost of 

**Fig. 9.4** Monthly income function 

**==> picture [133 x 118] intentionally omitted <==**

9.2 Some Application of Continuity to Problems in Economics 

207 

childcare, etc.), it is questionable how much this kind of a program motivates users of the program to actively look for a job. 

Therefore, there is a need for an alternative program which increases financial incentives to work. Consider the following program. Each unemployed individual still receives a fixed monthly payment of $1 500; however, if she finds a part-time job, 50% of the earnings is paid back to the program, while she keeps the remaining 50% Once she pays back $1 500 to the program, everything she earns above that amount is kept for herself. For example, if a person works 20 hours per month at a part-time job which pays $30 per hour, then $300 of the total of $600 earned is paid back to the program, while the remaining $300 is kept for herself. In addition, she receives a fixed monthly payment of $1 500 If she works 150 hours per month, then out of the total earnings of $4 500, $1 500 is paid back to the program (this amount is equal to the half of the total earnings in the first 100 hours of work), while the remaining amount is kept for herself. Therefore, $1 500 earned in the last 50 hours are entirely kept by the individual. The income of this person can be represented as the function of the number of hours worked, , within a given month:

that is,

Since 

**==> picture [197 x 15] intentionally omitted <==**

this function is obviously continuous at all points. Figure 9.5 shows the graph of this function. As this function is continuous, such program does motivate incentive to work. Also, certain funds are returned to the program and thus the cost of the program is reduced.

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

Differential calculus is one of the most important tools of mathematical analysis. It was discovered in the seventeenth century by two famous scientists—the Englishman Isaac Newton and the German Georg Wilhelm Leibnitz—who discovered it independently of each other. The foundation of single-variable differential calculus is the concept of a derivative, which shows how a change in the independent variable affects the change in the functional value, that is, the change in the dependent variable. Since in economics it is very important to establish this type of relationship between economic variables, differential calculus plays an important role in economic theory. For example, it is important to know how a change in quantity will affect costs, how a change in price will affect profit, how a change in the money supply will affect inflation, etc. In this chapter, we define the term derivative, derive rules of differentiation and show how to calculate it. We show how derivatives can simplify the calculation of limits of the indefinite form 0/0 and / , that is, we present the L’Hospital rule. After that, we introduce higher-order derivatives and conclude by presenting some economic applications. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Derivative · Differentiable function · Tangent line · Rules of differentiation · Chain rule · Implicit differentiation · Derivative of inverse function · Logarithmic differentiation · L’Hospital rule · Higher order derivatives · Economic applications

**Approximating Functions with Polynomials and Mean Value Theorems** 

**11** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

From the geometric interpretation of the derivative, the derivative is equal to the slope of the tangent line to the graph of a function at a point 0[In][other] words, at that point the function can be approximated by a tangent line, so the derivative shows by how much the functional value changes if the value of the independent variable increases from its current value 0[by][1,][where][instead] of the actual change in the value of the function, we observe the change in the value on the tangent line. In the same manner, we may wonder by how much the functional value will approximately change if we increase from its current value 0[by][some][arbitrarily][small amount] We describe this change by introducing the term differential. Moreover, we show how to approximate functions of one variable by polynomials. We start from linear approximations and arrive to Taylor’s formula and the Taylor polynomial of degree . Then we present the Bolzano-Weierstrass theorem. The importance of this theorem stems from the fact that it guarantees the existence of an equilibrium state or optimum for many economic models. We conclude by presenting two very important mean value theorems: Rolle’s theorem and Lagrange’s mean value theorem. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Differential · Linear approximation · Quadratic approximation · Taylor’s formula · Taylor polynomial of degree n · Weierstrass theorem · Rolle’s theorem · Lagrange’s mean value theorem

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

One of the most important applications of differential calculus in both mathematics and economics is finding points at which a function reaches a local minimum or maximum. In economics, for example, it is important to determine the basket of goods that maximizes utility, the level of production that maximizes profit, the input of labor (or capital) that minimizes the costs for a predetermined level of production, the level of production that minimizes average production costs, etc. Therefore, in this chapter we examine the problem of determining extrema of functions of one real variable. First, we introduce the concepts of local and global minima and maxima and then we use the first derivative to characterize and find intervals over which a function is increasing or decreasing, which will help us characterize and find extrema. Thus, we will obtain the necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of extrema using the first derivative. Since each local maximum is related to the concept of a concave function and each local minimum is related to the concept of a convex function, we will characterize the convexity and concavity of a twice differentiable function using the second derivative. We then derive sufficient conditions for the existence of extrema using the second derivative. This will help us develop an algorithm for determining all local extrema of any twice differentiable function. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Local extrema · Global extrema · Intervals over which a function is increasing or decreasing · Stationary points · Inflection points · Convex and concave functions · The first derivative test · The second derivative test · Algorithm for determining local extrema

In the examples shown, we could already see the basic relationship between the existence of local extrema at a point and the rise and fall of the function on the interval to the left and to the right of an extreme point. Thus, if a function at a point reaches a local minimum, then on some small interval 1 to the left of that point the function decreases, while on some small interval 1 to the right of the point the function increases. On the other hand, if is a local maximum point of a function , then on some small interval 1 to the left of the point the function increases, while on some small interval 1 to the right of the point it decrease. In other words, in order to determine extrema of a differentiable function , it is necessary to determine the points in which a function turns from an increasing into a decreasing one, and vice versa. Such points are candidates for extrema. However, it is difficult to determine the intervals over which a function is increasing or decreasing by using only the definition. Therefore, in the next chapter we show how to do it by using the derivative.

This theorem gives the procedure for finding local extrema of a continuous function which is differentiable at all points of the interval except perhaps at stationary points, by using only the first derivative: 

1. Find the domain of 

         - . 

2. Find points 1[,] 2[,] , at which 0 or at which is not defined, i.e., find stationary points. 

3. Split the domain of the function into intervals by using points 1[,] 2[,] , obtained in step 2 (for example, if _D_ , then these are the intervals 1 , 1 2 , , ). 

4. Within each interval, check the sign of , i.e., whether the function increases or decreases on that interval. 

   - (a) If throughout the interval immediately to the left of the stationary point the function strictly increases (i.e., 0), while throughout the interval immediately to the right of the point the function strictly decreases (i.e., 

      - 0), then is a strict local maximum point. 

   - (b) If throughout the interval immediately to the left of the stationary point the function strictly decreases (i.e., 0), while throughout the interval immediately to the right of the point the function strictly increases (i.e., 0), then is a strict local minimum point. 

   - (c) If throughout the interval immediately to the left of the stationary point the first derivative has the same sign as throughout the interval immediately to the right of the point , then is not a local extreme point.

## **12.4 Convex and Concave Functions** 

In the previous chapter, we saw how to find local extreme points using the first derivative. However, this procedure requires observing the behavior of a function on the interval immediately to the left and immediately to the right of a stationary point. The question arises whether it is possible to determine if a stationary point is also an extreme point by considering the behavior of a function only at that point. Concepts of convexity and concavity, i.e., their characterization using the second derivative in the case when a function is twice differentiable, will help us in this. But first, we introduce the concept of a convex combination of two points.

In other words, at an inflection point, the graph of a function changes its shape from a concave into a convex one, or from a convex into a concave one. If is strictly convex on 1 and strictly concave on 1 , then according to Theorems 12.13 and 12.14, its second derivative is positive on the interval 1 and negative on the interval 1 Since at the point the second derivative changes its sign, it must be 0, and since at the point the sign of the second derivative changes from positive into a negative one, is decreasing on some interval around . Similarly, if is concave on 1 and convex on 1 , then by Theorems 12.13 and 12.14, the sign of the second derivative on the interval 1 is negative, while its sign on the interval 1 is positive. In particular, at point we have 0 and the sign of the second derivative changes from negative to positive, so is increasing on some interval around . Therefore, the following two theorems hold. The first of them provides the necessary, while the second one provides the sufficient conditions for a point to be an inflection point.

In this chapter, we introduce some basic terms and properties related to cost, revenue and profit functions. We use differential calculus to derive some basic relationships among them. From the necessary condition for a point to be a local maximum point of the profit function we obtain the basic relationship that the maximum profit occurs at the point where marginal revenue equals marginal cost. Another important problem in economics is to determine how strongly a change in one economic variable will affect a change in another that is related to it. For example, we are interested in how intensively a change in price will affect a change in demand, a change in total revenues, etc. As we have seen so far, the term derivative allows us to determine what happens to the value of a dependent variable if we increase the value of an independent variable by one unit. However, clearly it is not the same whether the price of an ice cream increases by $1 or the price of a car increases by $1, given that the relative share of this increase in the total price of the product is significantly different. Therefore, it is often much more important to observe what will happen to the dependent variable (e.g. demand) when the independent variable (e.g. price) increases by 1%. This change is described by the term elasticity, which we consider in the last part of the chapter. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Cost function · Revenue function · Profit function · Average function · Marginal function · Elasticity · Marshall’s formula

## **13.2 Elasticity** 

One of the most important problems in economics is to determine how strongly a change in one economic variable will affect a change in another that is related to it. For example, we are interested in how intensively a change in price will affect a change in demand, a change in total revenues, etc. As we have seen so far, the term derivative allows us to determine what happens to the value of a dependent variable if we increase the value of an independent variable by one unit. However, clearly it is not the same whether the price of an ice cream increases by $1 or the price of a car increases by $1, given that the relative share of this increase in the total price of the product is significantly different. Therefore, it is often much more important to observe what will happen to the dependent variable (e.g., demand) when the independent variable (e.g., price) increases by 1% This change is described by the term elasticity. 

Let and let be a continuous function. We consider the ratio of the relative change in the dependent variable to the relative change in the independent variable , that is, the value of the expression

when the change in the independent variable is infinitesimally small, i.e., 0 Note that then also 0, since is a continuous function. In particular, if the

**Definition 13.4** Let and let be a differentiable function. We say that is 

- **Inelastic** with respect to if 1, that is, if 1 1 

- • **Elastic** with respect to if 1, that is, if 1 or 1 • **Unit elastic** with respect to if 1, that is, if 1 or 1 • **Perfectly inelastic** with respect to if 0 • **Perfectly elastic** with respect to if 

If is inelastic with respect to , then the percentage change in variable is less than the percentage change in variable . In other words, if increases by 1%, the variable will approximately change by less than 1% If is elastic with respect to , then the percentage change in variable is greater than the percentage change in variable . If increases by 1%, variable will approximately change by more than 1% 

In case of a unit elastic function, the percentage change in variable is equal to the percentage change in variable . In case of a perfectly inelastic function, the change in variable does not affect the change in the variable , that is, the value of the variable remains the same.

13.2 Elasticity 

315 

Let us find the limit of this expression as 0: 

**==> picture [236 x 25] intentionally omitted <==**

Note that, since is a continuous functions, 0 as 0, so also 0 0 The limit value of this expression is exactly equal to the elasticity of the demand function with respect to the change in price at the point 0[In][other] words, in case of a decreasing linear demand, the price elasticity of demand is equal to the ratio of the additional revenue earned and the revenue lost due to a change in price by an infinitesimally small amount Note that the elasticity of demand is negative, given that an increase in price results in a decrease in quantity. From this interpretation of the elasticity it can immediately be seen that if the demand function at the price level 0[is][elastic, i.e.,] 1, then a reduction in price will result in an increase in total revenue, since the amount of additional revenue earned as a consequence of increase in demand is greater than the amount of income lost due to reduction in price. Therefore, here, if one wants to increase the total revenue, one should reduce the price of a product, because the effect of an increase in demand will be greater than the effect of reducing the price. If the demand is inelastic, i.e., 

1 0, then the decrease in price will result in a decrease in total revenue, i.e., the effect of the increase in revenue due to the increase in demand will be smaller than the effect of the decrease in revenue due to the reduction of price. In this case, total revenues will increase if the price of the product increases. If the demand is unit elastic, i.e., 1, then a 1% price reduction will not affect the total revenue. Namely, in that case, the additional revenue earned due to the effect of increase in demand is exactly equal to the amount of revenue lost due to the effect of price reduction, so the total revenue remains the same. 

From this interpretation, the effect of increasing taxes on a product can also be predicted. Therefore, a government will increase its tax revenue by increasing tax on products whose demand is inelastic at a given price level. If it increases tax on products whose demand is elastic at a given price level, the total tax revenue will decrease.

## **13.3 Review Questions** 

1. Define the total cost function , as well as the average cost and the marginal cost functions. (a) Show that the minimum of the average cost function occurs at the point for which the marginal costs are equal to the average costs. 

   - (b) What should be the relationship between average and marginal costs so that the average cost function is increasing? Prove your claim. 

   - (c) What should be the relationship between average and marginal costs so that the average cost function is decreasing? Prove your claim.
