**Part III Differential Calculus of Functions of Several Variables** 

In the previous part, we considered functions of one variable and developed differential calculus for functions of one variable, which enabled a deeper understanding of the relationship between the dependent and the independent variable, as well as how a change in one variable affects another. However, in economic problems, the value of one variable usually does not depend only on the value of one other variable, but on several of them. Thus, for example, the demand for a product depends not only on the price of that product but also on the prices of some other products as well as income. The total cost of production depends not only on the level of production but also on the price of inputs. Therefore, in this part, we consider real functions of several real variables and develop differential calculus techniques for them. These techniques are a generalization of the differential calculus techniques for functions of one variable. This will enable us to establish how a change in one independent variable affects the change in the dependent variable, assuming that the remaining variables remain the same. A function of _n_ variables has _n_ partial derivatives which can be written using a vector we call the gradient, while the partial derivatives of the second order can be written using a symmetric matrix. Therefore, matrices play an important role here. Among other things, they enable us to establish the analogy between properties that apply to the differential calculus of functions of one variable and those which apply to the differential calculus of functions of several variables. Furthermore, the easiest way to check if a function is convex or concave is to check the properties satisfied by associated matrices. On the other hand, these properties enable us to derive the sufficient conditions for the existence of local extrema, with or without constraints. In addition, we will deal with homogeneous functions and establish a connection between homogeneity and elasticity. 

**==> picture [32 x 32] intentionally omitted <==**

**The Concept of a Function of Several Variables** 

**14** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

So far, we have considered functions of one variable and developed differential calculus for functions of one variable, which enabled a deeper understanding of the relationship between the dependent and independent variables, as well as how a change in one variable affects another. However, in economic problems, the value of one variable usually does not depend only on the value of one other variable, but on several of them. Thus, for example, the demand for a product depends not only on the price of that product, but also on the prices of some other products, as well as income. The total cost of production depends not only on the level of production, but also on the price of inputs, etc. Thus, in this chapter we introduce the notion of a function of several variables and present some basic terms related to it. We present some basic real functions of real variables, like linear function, monomials, and polynomials, and conclude by expanding the concept of a continuous function to functions of real variables.

Real function of real variables · Surface · Linear function · Monomial · Polynomial · Affine function · Continuous function

**Differential Calculus of Functions of Several Variables** 

**15** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

The differential calculus of functions of one variable made it possible to understand the relationship of how the change in the independent variable affects the change in the dependent variable , where However, in economic problems, the dependent variable is most often a function of several independent variables 1 2 , i.e. 1 2 Therefore, in this chapter we develop differential calculus for functions of several variables and thus deepen our understanding of the interrelationship among these variables. This will enable us to establish how a change in one independent variable affects a change in the dependent variable, assuming that the remaining variables remain the same. A function of variables has partial derivatives, which can be written using a vector we call the gradient, while the second-order partial derivatives can be written using a symmetric Hessian matrix. We continue by presenting the chain rule for functions of several variables, which enable us to find derivatives of composite functions, and then move on to the first order total differential and the equation of the tangent plane to a surface. Finally, by using the first order total differential, we derive the Implicit function theorem, which gives us the rule to differentiate implicitly defined functions. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Partial derivative · Gradient · Higher-order partial derivatives · Young’s theorem · Chain rule · First order total differential · Tangent plane · Implicit function theorem

**Homogeneous Functions** 

**==> picture [32 x 32] intentionally omitted <==**

**16** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

In this chapter we consider homogeneous functions. Functions with this property play an important role in economic theory. Thus, in standard economic models, profit functions and cost functions derived from production functions are naturally homogeneous functions, and the same is true for demand functions derived from utility functions. Furthermore, while differential calculus and the notion of the partial derivative allow us to establish a connection between the change in only one independent variable and the resulting change in the functional value, with all the other variables unchanged, for homogeneous functions we can estimate what happens to the functional value if values of all variables simultaneously change by the same percentage. In the case of homogeneous production functions, this leads to the concepts of increasing, decreasing and constant returns to scale. We also present some basic properties of homogeneous functions as well as present the very important Euler’s theorem. Finally, we introduce the notion of partial elasticity, which shows how strongly a function of variables 1 2 reacts to a change in the variable , assuming that the value of all the other variables is held constant. We show how elasticity is applied to a demand function to obtain the (own) price and cross price elasticity of demand, which enable us to determine if goods are substitutes or complements. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Homogeneous function · Degree of homogeneity · Linearly homogeneous function · Returns to scale · Radial expansion · Radial contraction · Partial · · elasticity (Own) Price elasticity of demand Cross price elasticity of demand · Substitutes · Complements

As by assumption is homogeneous of degree , we can establish a relationship between the value of at the new level 1 2 and the value of at the initial level 1 2 By definition, we have

It is important to emphasize that the percentage change in functional value is not related to the starting point 1 2 Regardless of the choice of the starting point 1 2 , whenever we increase all variables by the same percentage , the functional value will change by 100 1 % A function is said to be **linearly homogeneous** if it is homogeneous of degree 1 If is a linearly homogeneous function, then the simultaneous increase in value of all variables 1 2 by percentage results in the increase of the functional value by that same percentage , since 

**==> picture [171 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

Thus, for example, if we double the amount of all inputs of a linearly homogeneous production function, the amount of production will also double, regardless of the initial level of inputs 1 2 If we triple the quantities of all inputs, production will also triple. Note that the percentage change in production due to the increase of all inputs by the same percentage is not related to the initial input level. No matter what the initial input level, the same percentage change in inputs will lead to the same percentage change in the output. 

If 1, we say that a production function has **constant returns to scale** . In this case, an increase in all inputs by the same percentage results in an increase in the output by that same percentage , regardless of the initial level of inputs. Similarly, if the production function is homogeneous of degree 1, then an increase in all inputs by the same percentage will result in an increase in the output by a percentage that is less than , so in that case we say that the production function has **decreasing returns to scale** . If, on the other hand, the production function is homogeneous of degree 1, then an increase in all inputs by the same percentage will result in an increase in production by a percentage greater than , so in that

case we say that the production function has **increasing returns to scale** . Note that this change is independent of the initial level of inputs, but it depends only on the degree of homogeneity , in the sense that the same percentage change in the amount of inputs will always result in the same corresponding percentage change in the production function, regardless of the initial level of inputs. This means that when we simultaneously increase all the variables 1 2 by the same percentage , the functional value remains the same.

be the demand for good as a function of prices of goods 1 2 and income . Then, the **(own) price elasticity of demand** is equal to the partial elasticity of the demand function of good with respect to the change in the price of that very same good , that is, 

**==> picture [4 x 2] intentionally omitted <==**

It measures the direction and intensity of change in the demand for good due to the change in the price of that very same good by 1%, while all the other prices and income are held constant. 

The **cross price elasticity of demand** is equal to the partial elasticity of the demand function with respect to the change in the price of some other good , , and it is equal to . 

This coefficient measures the direction and intensity of change in the demand for good if the price of some other good , , increases by 1%, while all the other prices and income are held constant. The sign of cross price elasticity of demand tells us whether the goods are substitutes or complements. We say that two goods are **substitutes** if they could be used for the same purpose by customers. Such are, for example, a bus and a taxi, an android phone and an iPhone, etc. If two goods and are substitutes, then the demand for the product increases as the price of the product goes up, which means that the corresponding cross price elasticity of demand is positive, i.e., 

**==> picture [36 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

If the price of a given good increases, then the demand for its substitute increases as well. The higher this coefficient, the better substitutes the goods are, so they can be replaced more easily in use. 

16.3 Partial and Cross Elasticity 

399 

On the other hand, two goods are **complements** if the use of one good requires the consumption of the other good. Thus, for example, the use of a car requires the use of fuel. For complementary goods, the corresponding **cross price elasticity** of demand is negative, i.e., 

**==> picture [36 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

If the price of a good goes up, the demand for its complement goes down. 

The **income elasticity of demand** is equal to the partial elasticity of demand with respect to the change in income , that is, 

**==> picture [4 x 3] intentionally omitted <==**

It shows by what percentage the demand for the good will change if the income increases by 1%, while prices of all the other goods remain the same.

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

We have seen that the concepts of the convexity and the concavity of a function play an important role in determining sufficient conditions for the existence of local extrema of functions of one variable. The same is true for functions of several variables. Therefore, in this chapter we generalize these concepts to the case of functions of variables 1 2 Furthermore, we derive their characterization using differential calculus. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Convex set · Convex function · Concave function · Principal submatrix of order · Principal minor of order 

## **17.1 Basic Concepts** 

Before we introduce concepts of convexity and concavity of functions of variables 1 , first we introduce the concept of a convex set. 

**Definition 17.1** A set is said to be **convex** if for every two points **x** 1 and **y** 1 we have 

**x** 1 **y** for all 0 1 . 

In other words, a set is convex if for every two points **x** and **y** from the set , the segment

If in the relation (17.2) the sign of strict inequality holds, then we say that is a **strictly concave** function. 

Geometrically, a function of two variables 1 2 is concave if, for any choice of points **x y** , the segment connecting the points **x x** and **y y** is located below the graph of the function (Fig. 17.3). 

Note that a function of variables is convex (i.e., concave) if and only if the restriction of the function to any segment **x y** from the set is a convex (i.e., concave) function of one variable. 

## **17.2 Characterization of Convex and Concave Functions** 

Convexity and concavity of functions of one variable can be characterized using their first and second derivative. Thus, a function of one variable of class 1 is convex (concave) on an interval if and only if the function of its first derivative increases (decreases) on the interval . If is of class 2, then is convex (concave) on an interval if and only if 0 ( 0) for all 

In a similar way, it is possible to characterize convexity and concavity of functions of variables 1 2 by using their first- and second-order partial derivatives. Before we use the differential calculus to derive a criterion by which we can characterize these properties, we introduce the notion of the secondorder total differential. 

For this purpose, we introduce the following definition. 

**Definition 17.4** Let be a square matrix. The **principal submatrix of order** of the matrix is the matrix obtained by deleting the last rows and columns from the matrix . The determinant of the principal submatrix of order is called the **principal minor of order** of matrix .

**Optimization of Functions of Several Variables** 

**18** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

We have already seen the importance of optimization for functions of one variable. Since relations between economic variables are often described by functions of several variables, in economic theory the optimization of such functions is even more important. For example, a consumer chooses the basket of goods which maximizes his/her utility, a company selects the combination of inputs which produces a required level of output at minimal costs, etc. In this chapter we derive the necessary and sufficient conditions, also known as the first order and the second order conditions, for the existence of unconstrained extrema of functions of several variables. These conditions are generalizations of conditions that apply to functions of one variable. We state the algorithm for finding the unconstrained extrema of functions of several variables. Finally, we consider the problem of finding the extrema of functions of several variables with one constraint in the form of an equation and present the substitution method and the Lagrange multipliers method for solving it. Also, we derive the interpretation of the Lagrange multiplier. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Local extrema · Global extrema · Unconstrained optimization · The first order conditions · The second order condition · Constrained optimization · Substitution method · Lagrange multipliers method · Lagrange multiplier

In other words, if is strictly concave at a stationary point **x** , then has a strict local maximum at that point. In that case, every restriction of the function is also a strictly concave function, as shown in Fig. 18.15. Therefore, **x** is indeed a strict local maximum of . 

On the other hand, if is strictly convex at a stationary point **x** , then has a strict local minimum at that point. In that case, every restriction of the function is also a strictly convex function, as shown in Fig. 18.16. If **x** is a saddle point, then there is a restriction of the function which is a strictly convex function, but there is also a restriction which is a strictly concave function, as shown in Fig. 18.17.

## **18.3 Extrema of Functions of Several Variables with One Constraint in the Form of Equation** 

In optimization problems that occur in economics, constraints are often present. In this chapter, we consider how to determine local extrema of a function of several variables when there is a single constraint in the form of an equation. We encounter this type of problem when, for example, we have to find a combination of inputs which produces predetermined level of output with minimal production costs or we have to determine a basket of goods which maximizes utility for a given budget constraint. Here we will show two methods: the substitution method, which can be applied only to some constrained optimization problems, and the Lagrange multipliers method which gives a general procedure for finding local extrema of functions of variables with one constraint in the form of an equation. 

## **18.3.1 Substitution Method** 

We consider the problem of finding extrema of a function of two variables 1 2 with one constraint in the form of the equation 1 2 In a general form, this problem can be written as 1 2 min max subject to 1 2 . 

If it is possible to use the constraint to express one variable as a function of another in an explicit form, or if it is possible to use the constraint to express all terms containing one variable (e.g., 1[)][within][definition][of] in terms of another variable (i.e., 2[),][then][it][is][possible][to][apply][the][substitution][method.][It][consists][of][the] following: from the constraint, one variable is expressed in terms of another and then included back into the initial function. This transforms the problem of finding extrema of a function of two variables with one constraint into the problem of finding extrema of a function of one variable without constraints, which we know how to solve.

## **18.3.2 The Lagrange Multipliers Method** 

We consider the problem of finding local extrema of a function of two variables 1 2 subject to one constraint in the form of an equation

The set of all points satisfying the constraint (18.13) is called the **set of feasible solutions** to the problem (18.14). 

Similar to the substitution method, we want to convert this constrained optimization problem into an equivalent unconstrained optimization problem. In the substitution method, we used the constraint to express one variable in terms of another and thus reduced the number of variables by one. In this way we obtained the equivalent unconstrained optimization problem which has one less variable than the initial problem. In case of the Lagrange multipliers method, we will increase the number of variables by one and thus obtain an equivalent unconstrained optimization problem. 

**==> picture [333 x 47] intentionally omitted <==**

This function is called the **Lagrange function** . The variable is called the **Lagrange multiplier.** For all points 1 2 for which 1 2 , we have

Therefore, all stationary points of the function _L_ coincide with the stationary points of the function , and the constraint 1 2 holds. In other words, by introducing function _L_ 1 2 , the problem of finding extrema of a function of two variables with one constraint (18.14) is reduced to the unconstrained problem of finding stationary points of the Lagrange function of three variables _L_ 1 2 , without constraints. This increases the number of variables but removes the constraint.

**Applications of Multivariable Calculus in Economics** 

**19** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

Economics is the study of how individuals, businesses, and governments make decisions under conditions of scarcity. These decisions often involve optimizing certain outcomes—maximizing utility, minimizing costs, or maximizing profits—subject to various constraints like limited resources, budget restrictions, or technological capabilities. In this part we have seen the indispensable role of multivariable calculus in providing the mathematical framework for understanding and modeling these complex economic relationships. By extending the concepts of derivatives and optimization to functions of several variables, we have obtained powerful tools which enable us to analyze intricate interdependencies, derive optimal strategies, and predict economic behavior more accurately. From understanding consumer preferences and firm production decisions to modeling macroeconomic growth and policy interventions, the principles of multivariable calculus are foundational to modern economic theory and empirical analysis. Throughout this part of the book, we have already seen many applications in economics, however in this section we still review them and list some more applications. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Applications · Marginal effects · Optimization · Lagrange multipliers method · Utility maximization · Dual problem · Minimum expenditure problem · Profit maximization · Cost minimization for a given level of output · Optimizing policy instruments · Econometrics · Homogeneous functions 

The real world of economic phenomena is inherently multivariate. Economic agents typically face choices involving multiple variables simultaneously: consumers

Applications of Multivariable Calculus in Economics

19 

choose quantities of various goods, firms decide on multiple inputs for production, and policymakers consider numerous factors affecting the economy. 

The basic concepts of multivariable calculus are partial derivatives, gradient, second-order partial derivatives, and the Hessian matrix. Recall, for a function of several variables, _f (x_ 1 _, x_ 2 _, . . . , xn)_ , a partial derivative measures the rate of change of the function _f_ with respect to one variable while holding all the other variables constant. This is immensely useful in economics for analyzing marginal effects. For example, if a firm’s production function is _Q_ = _f (L, K)_ , where _Q_ is output, _L_ is labor, and _K_ is capital, then _[∂] ∂L[Q]_[represents][the][marginal][product][of][labor] ( _MP L_ ), indicating by how many units the output changes when labor increases by one unit, holding capital constant. In the same manner, _∂K[∂][Q]_[represents][the][marginal] product of capital ( _MP K_ ), indicating by how many units the output changes when capital increases by one unit, holding labor constant. The gradient of a function _f (x_ 1 _, x_ 2 _, . . . , xn)_ is a vector containing all of its first-order partial derivatives. It points in the direction of the steepest ascent of the function. In economics, the gradient is crucial for understanding the direction of maximum change in economic variables like utility or profit. 

Just as in single-variable calculus, second-order derivatives provide information about the curvature of a function. These second-order derivatives are organized into the Hessian matrix. We have seen that the Hessian matrix is vital for determining whether a critical point (where all first partial derivatives are zero) is a local maximum, minimum, or saddle point, which is fundamental for optimization problems in economics. 

To find the local extrema of a function _f (x_ 1 _, x_ 2 _, . . . , xn)_ , we first find the critical points by setting all first partial derivatives equal to zero: 

**==> picture [123 x 23] intentionally omitted <==**

The nature of these critical points (maximum, minimum, saddle) is then determined by analyzing the Hessian matrix using conditions related to its principal minors. 

Many economic problems involve optimizing an objective function subject to one or more constraints. For instance, a consumer maximizes utility subject to a budget constraint. The method of Lagrange multipliers is the primary tool for solving such problems. 

Recall, to optimize 

**==> picture [64 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [117 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

we form the Lagrangian function: 

**==> picture [283 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

where _λ_ is the Lagrange multiplier. We then find the critical points by setting all partial derivatives of _L_ (with respect to _xi_ . and _λ_ ) to zero. The Lagrange multiplier _λ_ often has an important economic interpretation as the shadow price of the constraint.

While microeconomic theory heavily relies on optimization, multivariable calculus also plays a significant role in macroeconomics. For example, governments and central banks often aim to optimize certain macroeconomic objectives (e.g., stable prices, low unemployment, economic growth) by adjusting policy instruments (e.g., interest rates, government spending, tax rates). These objectives can be represented as loss functions that depend on multiple economic variables. Multivariable calculus provides the framework for finding the optimal settings for these policy instruments. For instance, a central bank might minimize a loss function _L(π, u)_ = _(π_ − _π_[∗] _)_[2] + _β(u_ − _u_[∗] _)_[2] , where _π_ is inflation, _u_ is unemployment, and _π_[∗] and _u_[∗] are target levels. The bank would then use its policy tools to influence _π_ . and _u_ to achieve the minimum _L_ . 

Multivariable calculus also underpins many econometric techniques. For example, in multiple regression analysis, finding the ordinary least squares (OLS) estimators for regression coefficients involves minimizing the sum of squared residuals, which is a multivariable optimization problem. The partial derivatives of the sum of squared residuals with respect to each coefficient are set to zero to derive the normal equations. 

In this part, we have also dealt with homogeneous functions and elasticities of functions of several variables. As we have seen, these concepts provide a deeper understanding of how economic relationships scale and how sensitive one economic variable is to changes in another, especially when multiple factors are at play. Homogeneous functions are particularly useful for modeling production processes, cost structures, and consumer preferences where proportional changes in inputs lead to predictable proportional changes in output or utility. The degree of homogeneity directly relates to concepts like returns to scale in production. Elasticities, on the other hand, quantify the responsiveness of one variable to a percentage change in another, providing unit-free measures that are crucial for policy analysis, market forecasting, and understanding consumer behavior. Together, these tools allow economists to formalize and test hypotheses about the fundamental properties of economic functions and the behavior of economic agents. One of the most important results that we have presented here was the Euler’s theorem. It provides a powerful relationship between a homogeneous function and its partial derivatives. Recall, the Euler’s theorem states that if _f (x_ 1 _, x_ 2 _, . . . , xn)_ is homogeneous of degree _α_ , then 

**==> picture [222 x 23] intentionally omitted <==**

In economic terms, this means that the sum of the products of each input and its marginal product (or the sum of the value of marginal products if _f_ represents output) equals _α_ times the total output. For example, if _Q_ = _f (L, K)_ is a homogeneous production function of degree _α_ , then according to the Euler’s theorem, we have

that is, 

**==> picture [125 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

This theorem has profound implications for income distribution theory (e.g., how total output is distributed between labor and capital if factors are paid their marginal products). If _α_ = 1 (constant returns to scale), then 

**==> picture [119 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

implying that if factors are paid their marginal products, the entire output is exhausted. 

Elasticity, recall, is a unit-free measure of the responsiveness of one variable to a percentage change in another. Given a function _y_ = _f (x_ 1 _, x_ 2 _, . . . , xn)_ , the partial elasticity of _y_ with respect to _xi_ ., _Ey,xi_ ., measures the percentage change in _y_ for a 1% change in _xi_ ., holding all the other variables constant. For example, for a demand function 

**==> picture [111 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

of some good _i_ as a function of prices of _n_ goods _p_ 1 _, p_ 2 _, . . . , pn_ (including its own price _pi_ .) and income _Y_ , the own price elasticity of demand, _Eqi ,pi_ ., indicates how responsive quantity demanded is to changes in its own price. It also determines the impact of price changes on total revenue. On the other hand, the cross price elasticity of demand, _Eqi ,pj_ ., _i_ /= _j_ , measures the responsiveness of the quantity demanded of one good to a change in the price of another good. It is used to determine if goods are substitutes ( _Eqi ,pj >_ 0) or complements ( _Eqi ,pj <_ 0). Income elasticity of demand, _Eqi ,Y_ ., measures how quantity demanded changes with income. It is used to classify goods as normal ( _Eqi ,Y >_ 0), inferior ( _Eqi ,Y <_ 0), necessities (0 _< Eqi ,Y <_ 1), or luxuries ( _Eqi ,Y >_ 1). 

On the other hand, for a production function _Q_ = _f (L, K)_ , the partial elasticities are equal to 

**==> picture [133 x 53] intentionally omitted <==**

where _MP L_ and _MP K_ are marginal products of labor and capital, respectively. These elasticities measure the percentage change in output resulting from a 1% change in labor or capital, respectively. Notably, according to the Euler’s theorem, for a homogeneous production function of degree _α_ , the sum of these output 

19 Applications of Multivariable Calculus in Economics 

465 

elasticities equals the degree of homogeneity. This provides an alternative way to determine returns to scale: decreasing returns to scale if _α <_ 1, constant returns to scale if _α_ = 1, and increasing returns to scale if _α <_ 1 

Elasticities can often be simplified using logarithmic differentiation. If _y_ = _f (x_ 1 _, x_ 2 _, . . . , xn)_ , then 

**==> picture [114 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

The partial derivative of ln _y_ with respect to ln _xi_ . is precisely the elasticity: 

**==> picture [113 x 24] intentionally omitted <==**

This property is particularly useful in econometrics when dealing with log-linear models, where estimated coefficients directly represent elasticities. For instance, in a model ln _Q_ = _β_ 0 + _β_ 1 ln _L_ + _β_ 2 ln _K_ , we have _β_ 1 = _EQ,L_ and _β_ 2 = _EQ,K_ . 

There’s a close relationship between homogeneous functions and elasticities, especially concerning returns to scale. As shown, the sum of output elasticities with respect to inputs equals the degree of homogeneity for a production function. This provides a direct link between the local responsiveness of output to inputs (elasticities) and the global scaling properties of the production process (homogeneity/returns to scale). 

Furthermore, in specific contexts like demand functions, homogeneity of degree zero with respect to prices and income (absence of money illusion) implies certain relationships between own price, cross price, and income elasticities (e.g., Cournot aggregation, Engel aggregation), which are crucial for empirical demand analysis. 

As we have seen, homogeneous functions provide a robust framework for analyzing scaling properties, particularly returns to scale in production and consumption. Euler’s theorem offers a powerful theoretical link between marginal products and total output for homogeneous functions, with significant implications for income distribution. Elasticities, on the other hand, provide unit-free measures of responsiveness, enabling economists to quantify the sensitivity of economic variables to changes in other factors. From understanding consumer behavior through various demand elasticities to analyzing factor substitutability in production, elasticities are invaluable for empirical work, policy design, and market analysis. Together, these tools form an essential part of the mathematical apparatus for economists, allowing for a more thorough and quantitative understanding of complex economic phenomena. 

As you can see, the differential calculus of functions of several variables represents an indispensable tool for modern economists. It provides the rigorous mathematical foundation necessary to model and analyze complex economic phenomena involving multiple interacting variables. From the microeconomic decisions of consumers and firms to the macroeconomic dynamics of growth and policy, the concepts of partial derivatives, gradients, Hessians, and constrained optimization techniques like Lagrange multipliers, as well as concepts of homogeneous functions 

Applications of Multivariable Calculus in Economics 

466 

19 

and elasticity, are central to understanding how economic agents make optimal choices and how economic systems evolve. While advanced economic analysis may use more sophisticated mathematical tools, multivariable calculus remains the bedrock, enabling economists to translate intuitive economic ideas into precise, solvable mathematical problems, thereby deepening our understanding of the economic world. 

**Part IV Integrals and Differential Calculus** 

In this part, we deal with the question of how to determine a function if we know its derivative. So, for example, given a marginal cost function, we want to know what is the corresponding total cost function. Or, given a marginal revenue function, we want to know what is the corresponding total revenue function. The set of all functions _F(x)_ that have the property that their derivative is equal to some function _f (x)_ is called an indefinite integral. For a given function _f (x)_ , its indefinite integral is obtained by a procedure that is opposite to the procedure of finding its derivative, and therefore _F(x)_ is also called an antiderivative or a primitive function. In addition to the term indefinite integral, there is also the term definite integral, where the definite integral of a function _f (x)_ is equal to the signed area enclosed by the graph of the function _f (x)_ and the _x_ -axes on an interval [ _a, b_ ] Thus, for example, for a given marginal cost function, the total cost at the production level _q_ is equal to the area under the marginal cost function on the interval [0 _, q_ ] In addition to indefinite and definite integrals, as well as techniques of their calculation, we will also consider differential equations of the first order. 

**==> picture [32 x 32] intentionally omitted <==**

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

In this chapter, we deal with the question of how to determine a function if we know its derivative. So, for example, given a marginal cost function, we want to know its corresponding total cost function. Or, given a marginal revenue function, we want to know its corresponding total revenue function. The set of all functions that have the property that their derivative is equal to some function is called an indefinite integral. For a given function , its indefinite integral is obtained by a procedure that is opposite to the procedure of finding its derivative, and therefore is also called an antiderivative or a primitive function. We show three methods of integration: direct integration, integration by substitution, and integration by parts. In addition to the term indefinite integral, there is also the term definite integral, where the definite integral of a function is equal to the signed area enclosed by the graph of the function and the -axes on an interval Thus, for example, for a given marginal cost function, the total cost at the production level is equal to the area under the marginal cost function on the interval 0 We apply the concept of the definite integral to the problem of finding the area enclosed by some curves and conclude by improper integrals, that is, integrals in which either the upper or lower bound of integration, or both,

## **20.2 Methods of Integration** 

In all the examples shown so far, we were able to calculate integrals by direct application of table of integrals and basic rules of integration. Therefore, this procedure is also called the **direct integration** . It can be applied in the case when the subintegral function can be written as a sum or a difference of functions (possibly multiplied by a constant) given in the table of integrals. 

However, this is most often not the case, so we need some additional methods to help us determine indefinite integrals. In this chapter, we will show two such methods: integration by substitution and integration by parts. 

## **20.2.1 Integration by Substitution** 

This method can be applied if one part of the subintegral function can be replaced by a new variable , whereby the rest of the subintegral function can be expressed as a product of the differential and some function of the variable and thus obtain a new subintegral function that allows the application of the direct integration. In other words, after introducing a new variable, both the new subintegral function and the differential must be expressed exclusively in terms of the variable . Once we find the primitive function of the new subintegral function, it is still necessary to express everything in terms of the initial variable . 

Before we formally describe the method, let us look at the following example.

## **20.3 Definite Integral**

**Differential Equations** 

**==> picture [32 x 32] intentionally omitted <==**

**21** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

In economic models, often it is important to know how the value of an economic variable changes over time. The relationship between variables is often described by a single equation or a system of equations that include the variables and also their derivatives. In the case when the value of these variables is observed in continuous time, we talk about differential equations. Differential equations are equations that relate the independent variable and the unknown function with the property that they contain at least one derivative of the function or the differentials and . The order of the highest derivative (or differential) appearing in a given differential equation is called the order of that differential equation. In this chapter, we deal only with linear differential equations and show methods to solve them in the case of both the homogeneous and nonhomogeneous linear differential equations. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Differential equation · General solution · Particular solution · First-order linear differential equation · Homogeneous differential equation · Nonhomogeneous differential equation · Separable differential equation · Variation of constants method

**Application of Integrals and Differential Equations in Economics** 

**22** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

Integrals and differential equations allow economists to study how quantities accumulate over time or across different levels, as well as to model accumulation, total change, and dynamic processes over time. Integrals are crucial for moving from marginal concepts to total quantities, such as calculating total cost from marginal cost or consumer and producer surplus in market analysis. We do this by calculating the corresponding definite integral, which can be interpreted as the area under a curve between two points. This seemingly simple geometric idea unlocks profound insights into various economic phenomena, since the area under the curves of demand functions, supply functions, cost functions, revenue functions, and so on often represents a total quantity, a cumulative value, or a measure of welfare. For instance, the area between a demand curve and a price level can represent the total benefit consumers receive beyond what they pay, the area under a marginal cost curve represents the total variable cost of production, etc. Differential equations, on the other hand, are the language of change and evolution. They enable economists to model how economic variables evolve over time, describing dynamic systems such as economic growth, market adjustments, and the accumulation of capital. In this chapter, we list some of these applications, including the ones we have already seen throughout this part, thus illustrating how integrals and differential equations provide powerful insights into economic behavior and system dynamics. We start by showing how to compute total cost (revenue) from marginal cost (revenue) function and how to compute capital stock at time given the rate of net investment and initial capital stock 0 Then we present the application of integrals to the calculation of producer surplus, consumer surplus, and total economic surplus. We continue by discussing the Lorenz curve and the Gini coefficient, which are instrumental in quantifying income or wealth inequality. Another application of integrals is the calculation of the present value of continuous future income given a continuous interest rate. As for the application of differential equations in 

© The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026 Z. Lukaˇc, _Economic Analysis Through Mathematics_ , Springer Texts in Business and Economics, https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-08812-3_22 

529 

Application of Integrals and Differential Equations in Economics 

530 

22 

economics, we start with the Solow-Swan growth model, which is a cornerstone of neoclassical growth theory. It explains long-run economic growth by looking at capital accumulation, labor or population growth, and increases in productivity (technological progress). We move on to the price adjustment model, which uses differential equations to model how markets adjust to equilibrium over time. Another application of differential equations deals with investment and capital stock dynamics and shows how the capital stock evolves over time. Given an initial capital stock and a path for investment, one can solve for the time path of capital stock. This equation is crucial for understanding capital accumulation and its role in production. We conclude by an application of differential equations to financial mathematics in which we derive the formula for continuous compounding. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Applications · Total cost · Marginal cost · Total revenue · Marginal revenue · Capital stock · Net investment rate · Producer surplus · Consumer surplus · Total economic surplus · Lorenz curve · Gini coefficient · Present value at continuous annual interest rate · Solow-Swan growth model · Price adjustment model · Time path of capital stock · Continuous compounding 

Recall, integration is the inverse operation of differentiation, and it is used to find the total quantity or sum when the rate of change is known. In economics, this often means moving from marginal concepts to total values. Thus, we can compute total cost from marginal cost, total revenue from marginal revenue, etc. For example, if a firm’s marginal cost function is given by , where denotes total costs, then the total variable cost ( ) of producing 0[units][can][be][found] by integrating the marginal cost function:

In the examples presented in this part, we have already encountered producer surplus and producer surplus. Recall, **producer surplus** ( ) is the economic measure of the benefit producers receive when they sell a product at a market price that is higher than the minimum price they were willing to accept. It is the difference between what producers receive and their marginal cost of production (or their minimum acceptable price). Graphically, the supply curve, , represents the minimum price producers are willing to accept for each quantity of a good (which is often related to their marginal cost). If the market price is 0[and][the] corresponding quantity supplied is 0[,][then][producers][who][were][willing][to][s][upply at] prices below 0[receive][a][surplus.][The][producer][surplus][(] ) is represented by the area between the supply curve and the horizontal line representing the market price 0[,][from] 0 to 0[If][the][supply][function is] , and the market price is 0[at][which] 0[units][are][sold,][the][producer][surplus][i][s given by]

This second form shows that producer surplus is the total revenue ( 0 0[)][minus][the] total variable cost or minimum revenue required (the integral of the supply curve). Producer surplus is used to analyze the welfare of producers in a market.

On the other hand, **consumer surplus** ( ) is an economic measure of the extra benefit or satisfaction (utility) consumers receive when they pay less for a product than the maximum price they were willing to pay. It’s the difference between what consumers are willing to pay and what they actually pay. Graphically, the demand curve, , represents the maximum price consumers are willing to pay for each quantity of a good. If the market price is 0[and][the][corresponding][quantity] demanded is 0[,][then][consumers][who][were][willing][to][pay][more][than] 0[receive] a surplus. The consumer surplus is represented by the area between the demand curve and the horizontal line representing the market price 0[,][from] 0 to 0[If][the][demand function is] , and the market price is 0[at][which] 0 units are sold, the consumer surplus is given by

**Total economic surplus** (also known as total welfare or social surplus) is the sum of consumer surplus and producer surplus in a market. 

**==> picture [110 x 10] intentionally omitted <==**

In a perfectly competitive market, the equilibrium price and quantity occur where the demand and supply curves intersect. At this equilibrium, total economic surplus is maximized. This is one of the fundamental arguments for the efficiency of competitive markets. Graphically, total surplus is the area between the demand curve and the supply curve, up to the equilibrium quantity 

**==> picture [161 x 24] intentionally omitted <==**

Any quantity other than (or any price other than ) will result in a lower total surplus, leading to a deadweight loss, which represents the loss of economic efficiency. Integrals are also instrumental in quantifying income or wealth inequality. The **Lorenz curve** and the **Gini coefficient** are standard tools for this purpose. A Lorenz curve, , is a graphical representation of the distribution of income or wealth. It plots the cumulative percentage of total income received against the cumulative 

22 Application of Integrals and Differential Equations in Economics 

533 

percentage of recipients, starting from the poorest. If represents the cumulative proportion of the population (from 0 to 1, or 0% to 100%), then represents the cumulative proportion of total income earned by that proportion of the population. Here, a perfectly equal distribution would be represented by a straight line (the line of perfect equality), where, for example, the bottom 10% of the population earns 10% of the total income. However, in reality, Lorenz curves are typically bowed beneath the line of perfect equality, indicating that the bottom % of the population earns less than % of the total income. The Gini coefficient (or Gini index) is a measure of statistical dispersion intended to represent the income or wealth inequality within a nation or any other group of people. It is defined as a ratio with values between 0 and 1. Here, a Gini coefficient of 0 represents perfect equality (everyone has the same income), while a Gini coefficient of 1 (or 100%) represents maximal inequality (one person has all the income, and all others have none). 

The Gini coefficient is calculated as the ratio of the area between the line of perfect equality and the Lorenz curve to the total area under the line of perfect equality (which is always 0 5 since it’s a triangle with base 1 and height 1). Many investments or assets generate a continuous stream of income over time. To determine the current worth of such an income stream, we need to calculate its present value. This involves discounting future income because money received in the future is worth less than money received today (due to factors like inflation, opportunity cost, and risk). If is the rate of income flow at time (e.g., dollars per year), and is the continuous annual discount rate (interest rate), the present value ( ) of this income stream from time 0 to is given by 

**==> picture [73 x 26] intentionally omitted <==**

The term is the continuous discount factor. If the income stream is perpetual (continues forever, ), the present value is 

**==> picture [75 x 25] intentionally omitted <==**

Differential equations, on the other hand, describe the relationship between a function and its derivatives, making them ideal for modeling dynamic economic processes where variables change over time. 

Simple macroeconomic growth models often use differential equations to describe the evolution of key variables like capital stock or output. One of such models is **Solow-Swan growth model** , which is a cornerstone of the neoclassical growth theory. It explains long-run economic growth by looking at capital accumulation, labor or population growth, and increases in productivity (technological progress). Let be the aggregate capital stock, be the labor force, and be the level of technology (or “effectiveness of labor”). Let 

be the capital per effective worker, and let

The term represents actual investment per effective worker, and represents the “breakeven” investment required to keep constant (i.e., to equip new workers, account for technological progress making existing capital relatively less productive, and replace depreciated capital). In the steady state, output per worker ( ) grows at the rate of technological progress , and total output grows at the rate As you can see, solving this differential equation allows economists to find the steady-state level of capital and analyze the dynamics of economic growth. Differential equations can also model how markets adjust to equilibrium over time, rather than assuming instantaneous adjustment. One such model is the **price adjustment model** . It assumes that the rate of change of price ( ) is proportional to the excess demand ( ), that is,

where 0 is an adjustment coefficient. This first-order differential equation describes how prices evolve toward equilibrium. If , price increases; if , price decreases. 

Another application of differential equations deals with investment and capital stock dynamics in presence of depreciation rate. Let denote the capital stock at time , let denote the new investment at time , and let denote depreciation rate. Now, the change in the capital stock ( ) is determined by new investment ( ) minus depreciation ( ): 

This first-order linear differential equation shows how the capital stock evolves over time, and given an initial capital stock and a path for investment, one can solve for the time path of capital stock. This equation is crucial for understanding capital accumulation and its role in production. 

Differential equations are also used in financial mathematics, especially in the context of continuous compounding and discounting (we will talk about mathematics of finance in the last part of the book). For example, if an amount 0[grows][at][a] continuous rate , the rate of change of that amount is equal to

22 Application of Integrals and Differential Equations in Economics

The solution to this differential equation is 0 , which is the formula for continuous compounding. This integral is derived from the concept of continuous discounting, which itself stems from the differential equation for continuous compounding. 

As we can see, integrals and differential equations of single variable are foundational mathematical tools that provide deep insights into various economic phenomena. Integrals enable economists to aggregate marginal effects into total quantities, crucial for welfare analysis, cost determination, and revenue calculation. Differential equations, on the other hand, are indispensable for modeling dynamic processes, allowing for the analysis of how economic variables evolve over time, adjust to equilibrium, and contribute to long-run growth. Together, these singlevariable calculus concepts form an essential part of the economist’s toolkit, enabling a rigorous and dynamic understanding of economic systems. 

**Part V Mathematics of Finance** 

Transactions involving money form the basis of modern economy. As the common proverb claims, “money makes the world go round.” One of the fundamental properties of money is that it has time value: $100 today is not the same as $100 a year ago, or $100 a year from now. The reason for this is that money can be invested and thus earn more money, that is, it can earn interest. In this chapter, we deal with an interest and different methods to compute interest. Here, we will just briefly mention the simple interest. The focus will be on the compound interest and its application to annuities and amortization schedules. 

**==> picture [32 x 32] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Interest** 

**23** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

Transactions involving money form the basis of the modern economy. One of the fundamental properties of money is that it has time value: $100 today is not the same as $100 a year ago, or $100 a year from now. The reason for this is that money can be invested and thus earn more money, that is, it can earn interest. In this chapter, we introduce the basic terminology regarding interest as well as different methods to compute interest. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Principal · Interest · Rate of interest · Term · Amount · Accumulated value · Maturity value · Discount rate · Interest rate · Discount interest · Simple interest · Compound interest 

In any business transaction involving borrowing or lending money, there are two parties involved–an investor and a debtor. An investor is lending money to a debtor, who must pay back the amount originally borrowed, called the **principal** , and the fee charged for the use of money, called the **interest** (Fig. 23.1). 

Therefore, interest has two interpretations. From an investor’s point of view, it is an income from an invested capital (because lending money is a form of investment), while from the borrower’s point of view, it is the price of money, that is, a price a borrower pays for the use of money he/she does not own. Depending on the context, the sum of the principal and the interest is called the **amount** or **accumulated value** or **maturity value** (Fig. 23.2). 

**Rate of interest** is the ratio of the interest earned in one time unit to the principal, as shown in Fig. 23.3. 

The time period from the moment money has been borrowed till the moment it has been repaid is called the **term** . Interest can be paid either at the beginning or 

> © The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026 Z. Lukaˇc, _Economic Analysis Through Mathematics_ , Springer Texts in Business and Economics, https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-08812-3_23 

539 

23 Interest 

540 

**==> picture [265 x 100] intentionally omitted <==**

**Fig. 23.1** Business transaction involving borrowing or lending money 

**Fig. 23.2** Amount, i.e., accumulated or maturity value 

**==> picture [121 x 82] intentionally omitted <==**

**Fig. 23.3** Rate of interest 

**==> picture [121 x 54] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [289 x 97] intentionally omitted <==**

**Fig. 23.4** Discount interest 

at the end of the term. If it is being paid at the beginning of the term, then we talk about a **discount rate.** Here, the amount the borrower receives is equal to the loan less the interest on the loan, that is, the interest on the loan is deducted from the loan up front. At the end of the term, the full amount of the loan is repaid (Fig. 23.4). 

On the other hand, if an interest is being paid at the end of the term, then we talk about an **interest rate.** Here, the amount the borrower receives is exactly equal to the loan, while at the end of the term, principal plus the interest is repaid (Fig. 23.5). Another classification of interest is with respect to what earns interest: does only the principal earns interest, or does interest also earns interest. If only the principal 

23 Interest 

541 

**==> picture [289 x 98] intentionally omitted <==**

**Fig. 23.5** Interest rate 

earns interest, then the interest per each period is calculated as a percentage of the principal. In this case we talk about the **simple interest** . If the interest earned in the previous periods also earns interest, then we talk about the **compound interest** . 

Given that there are two possibilities of when to pay the interest (up front or at the end of a term) as well as two possibilities of what earns interest (only the principal, or the interest earned so far also earns interest), we have the following four possibilities for the interest rate: simple interest rate, simple discount rate, compound interest rate, and compound discount rate. We will only consider the simple interest rate and the compound interest rate. 

We start with the simple interest rate. 

**==> picture [32 x 32] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Simple Interest** 

**24** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

In this chapter, we give a brief overview of simple interest at a simple interest rate. If simple interest rate is used, then for each period, an interest per period is computed as a percentage of the amount originally borrowed, and it is paid at the end of the term. Therefore, an interest per period is constant, regardless of the number of periods. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Simple interest · Principal · Present value · Accumulated value · Maturity value · Accumulation factor at a simple interest rate · Discount factor at a simple interest rate · Discounted value · Present value · Exact simple interest · Ordinary simple interest · Banker’s rule · Exact time · Approximate time 

If simple interest at a simple interest rate is used, then for each period, an interest per period is computed as a percentage of the amount originally borrowed, and it is paid at the end of the term. Therefore, the interest per period is constant, regardless of the number of periods (Fig. 24.1). Figure 24.1 illustrates simple interest in the case of three periods. It highlights the fact that an interest earned per period is always equal to the same amount, regardless of a period. However, Fig. 24.1 might be a little bit misleading, because interest per period is not being paid at the end of the corresponding period, but at the end of the term, that is, the total amount of the interest (sum of interests earned in each period) is being paid at the end of the term, at the same time as the amount originally borrowed is repaid. 

The amount originally borrowed is called the **principal** . However, depending on the context, it is also referred to as the **present value** or the discounted value of the amount due at the end of the term. The amount due at the end of the term is equal to the sum of the principal and the total interest. Depending on the context, it is called

The time obtained in the last example is 3 _._ 5 years or 42 months. How many days is that? If we deposit money today, on which date can we come and collect the money? Well, the answer depends on the method we use to count the number of days and on how many days a year is assumed to have. 

Simple interest is mostly used in business transactions in which the time _n_ is less than 1 year. Therefore, _n_ is usually a fraction of a year. The question arises how to get that number or how to convert that number into number of days, since different months have different number of days and not every year has the same number of days. If we assume that each month has 30 days, then this implies that a year has 360 days. However, the exact number of days in a year is either 365 or 366, depending on whether it is a “normal” or a “leap” year. There is also a question of how to compute the number of days between the two dates. Again, there are two possibilities: either we compute the exact number of days, or we find the approximate number of days, using the assumption that each month has 30 days. As we see, things can get complicated. 

If time is given in months, we compute _n_ as 

**==> picture [99 x 22] intentionally omitted <==**

If time is given in days, then two varieties are used in applications of simple interest—either a year has 365 days or a year has 360 days. If we assume that a year has 365 days, then we refer to it as the **exact simple interest** . In this case, the time is calculated as 

**==> picture [87 x 22] intentionally omitted <==**

If we assume that a year has 360 days, then we refer to it as the **ordinary simple interest** , and we calculate the time as 

**==> picture [87 x 22] intentionally omitted <==**

The ordinary simple interest is also referred to as the **Banker’s rule** .

Note that the amount of interest obtained for the ordinary simple interest is always greater than the amount of interest obtained for the exact simple interest (because in the first case we divide by 360, and in the second case we divide by 365). Therefore, the ordinary simple interest brings increased revenue to the lender! 

As for calculating the number of days between the two dates, again there are two methods. The first one computes the exact number of days, including all days except the first one, and therefore it is called the **exact time** . It takes the serial numbers of the starting date and the maturity date and find their difference, where the serial number of the starting date is subtracted from the serial number of the maturity date. Therefore, it counts the last day of the term, but it doesn’t count the first day. Serial numbers of dates are given in the serial table given in Fig. 24.4. In case of a leap year, the serial number of a date after February 28 is obtained by adding 1 to the serial number given in a serial table. 

The second method assumes that every months has 30 days. This method is called the **approximate time.** Here, the number of days is obtained in the following way. For a given starting date, first we find the number of months till the same day in the month containing the maturity date, then multiply the such obtained number of months by 30, and finally add the exact number of days for the remaining time, or subtract for the extra time. For example, if the starting date is March 5 and the maturity date is July 30, the number of months from March 5 till July 5 is 4 . Since from July 5 till July 30 there are exactly 25 days, the approximate time is equal to 4 · 30 + 25 = 145 days. Note that in this case the exact number of days is equal to 147. If the starting date is again March 5 and the maturity date is July 2, the number of months from March 5 till July 5 is 4 . Since from July 2 till July 5 there are 3

**Example 24.7** Find the exact time and the approximate time from April 21, 2022, to October 4, 2022. 

24 Simple Interest 

551 

**Solution** Let us first compute the exact time. The year 2022 is not a leap year, so the serial number of the maturity date, October 4, is 277. The serial number of the starting date, April 21, is 111. Therefore, the exact time is equal to 277 − 111 = 166 days. 

As for the approximate time, first we compute the number of months from April 21 to October 21, which is equal to 6, and then we find the exact number of days from October 4 to October 21, which is equal to 17 Therefore, the approximate time is equal to 6 · 30 − 17 = 163 days.

**Compound Interest**

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

Compound interest is the most widely used form of interest in business transactions. Here, the interest earned in one period also earns interest in the following period. Therefore, the speed of accumulation over time is much faster than that of simple interest. We start by deriving the fundamental compound interest formula, and then we discuss what to do if the interest conversion period does not coincide with the nominal interest rate. We show how to compute equivalent interest rate and effective interest rate. Next, we consider continuous compounding, that is, what happens if the number of interest conversion periods grows to infinity. One of the common problems in business practice is to find the principal which accumulates to an amount . Therefore, we consider discounted value, both for the case of discretely and continuously compounded interest. Then we show how to find the rate (given principal , accumulated value , and time ), as well as how to find time (given principal , accumulated value , and interest rate ). We conclude the chapter by showing how to compare cash flows due at different date, which leads us to the concept of equivalent amounts and equations of value. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Compound interest · Conversion period · Fundamental compound interest formula · Accumulation factor · Nominal interest rate · Frequency of conversion · Annual effective interest rate · Effective interest rate per period · Geometric mean rate of return · Continuous compounding · Force of interest · Discounted value · Finding rate · Finding time · Equivalent amounts · Equations of value

## **25.1 Fundamental Compound Interest Formula** 

Compound interest is the most widely used form of interest in business transactions. Here, the interest earned in one period also earns interest in the following period. Therefore, the speed of accumulation over time is much faster than that of the simple interest. 

Let us compare what happens if we accumulate $1,000 over 3 years at the simple and at the compound interest at the same annual rate of 10%. If the simple interest is used, then the principal of $1,000 earns $100 interest in the first year, $100 interest in the second year, and $100 interest in the third year, that is, the interest per period is always equal to 1 000 0 1 100, since it is always calculated as 10% of the principal. The accumulated value at the end of the third year is equal to

On the other hand, in the case of the compound interest, interest earns interest. Therefore, the $100 interest earned in the first year also earns interest in the second year, so the interest earned in the second year is equal to 0 1 1 000 100 110 In the third year, both $100 interest earned in the first year and $110 interest earned in the second year earn interest, so the interest earned in the third year is equal to 

**==> picture [137 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

Therefore, the accumulated value at the end of the third year is equal to 

**==> picture [145 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

Note that here the compound interest is greater than the simple interest. Figure 25.1 compares the two methods. 

This example shows that with the compound interest, the interest earned in a given period is added to the principal at the end of that period, and then it earns 

**==> picture [331 x 114] intentionally omitted <==**

**Fig. 25.1** Comparison of simple and compound interest 

25.1 Fundamental Compound Interest Formula 

555 

interest in subsequent periods. The sum of the principal and the total interest earned, that is, the amount due at the end of the term, is called the **compound amount** or the **accumulated value** . **Compound interest** is equal to the difference between the accumulated value and the principal. **Conversion period** or the **compounding period** is the time between two successive interest calculations. As with the simple interest, the **term** denotes the time from the moment the money has been borrowed till the moment it has been repaid. For example, if the interest is computed at the end of each month for 2 years, then the conversion period is a month, and the term is 2 years. 

Let us now derive the fundamental compound interest formula. We use the following notation: 

- : original principal or the present value or the discounted value of 

- : compound amount of , or accumulated value of 

- : compound interest, 

- : total number of interest (or conversion) periods involved 

- : rate of interest per conversion period 

Also, let denote the compound amount at the end of the interest conversion period . During period 1, the principal earns interest , so the compound amount 1[at][the][end][of][period][1][is equal to]

## **25.2 Equivalent Compound Interest Rates** 

Rate of interest is usually stated as an annual rate of interest. We call such an annual rate the **nominal rate of interest** . Very often, for a given nominal interest rate, interest is computed at the end of periods shorter than 1 year. For example, loan payments are usually made at the end of each month, although a loan is taken at a nominal annual rate. The question arises—what interest rate should be applied to this shorter interest conversion period? Let denote the number of interest conversion periods per year. The number is also called the **frequency of conversion** or the **frequency of compounding** . Common values of are 1 (annual compounding, i.e., interest is calculated at the end of every year), 2 (semiannual compounding, i.e., interest is calculated at the end of every 6 months), 4 (quarterly compounding, i.e., interest is calculated 4 times a year, at the end of every 3 months), 52 (weekly compounding, i.e., interest is calculated at the end of every week), 365 (daily compounding), and , that is,

Note that in the previous example, although the nominal interest rate remained the same, there was a huge difference in the amount . The higher the frequency of conversion , the greater the amount for the same nominal interest rate. As a matter of fact, the difference in for 12 and for 1 amounts to 15 656 81 15 386 24 270 57! This conclusion also holds in general. The higher the frequency of conversion for the same nominal interest rate, the greater the interest and the accumulated amount. If the frequency of conversion affects the amount of interest paid, is there a way to tell what is the actual annual interest rate that we’re paying? With such a rate, the interest would be compounded only once, that is, at the end of a year, and the amount would be the same as the one obtained by , that is, when interest is converted times a year. We call such a rate the **annual effective interest rate** . Therefore, for a given nominal rate of interest (compounded times per year), we define the **annual effective interest rate** to be the rate which, if compounded annually, will yield the same amount of interest per year. Also, two nominal rates with different frequencies of conversion are said to be **equivalent** if they yield the same accumulated value at the end of 1 year (and, hence, at the end of any number of years). 

Let us derive the formula for the annual effective interest rate. We consider what happens with the principal if it is invested for 1 year at rate (with interest compounded times per year and the interest rate per period being equal to ) and if it is invested at the rate for which the interest is compounded only once at the end of the year. If these two rates are equivalent, then the accumulated value at the end of the year will be the same for both of them (Fig. 25.7). Now we have 1 1 1 1 ,

## **25.6 Equations of Value** 

We have already seen that in order to be able to compare cash flows due at different dates, we have to accumulate or discount them to the same date. Note that, in doing this, we have implicitly assumed that, at an interest rate , principal is equivalent to the amount 1 due periods later, and vice versa. In this chapter, we formalize the concept of equivalence. 

We say that an amount due on a given date is **equivalent** at a given compound interest rate to the amount due periods later, if 

**==> picture [206 x 23] intentionally omitted <==**

Figure 25.13 illustrates the relationship of equivalence. 

Note that if we move forward in time, we need to accumulate, that is, multiply with the corresponding accumulation factor 1 If we move backward in time, we need to discount, that is, multiply with the corresponding discount factor 1 1 If in different interest conversion periods we have different interest rates, then for each period that we move forward (backward), we have to multiply by the corresponding accumulation factor 1 (discount factor 11 ), where is the interest rate per that period. Figure 25.14 illustrates this relationship. 

The relationship of equivalence defined by (25.21) satisfies the property of transitivity, that is, if at a given compound interest rate the amount is equivalent to , and is equivalent to , then is equivalent to . ## **Simple Annuities** 

**26** 

## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

In this chapter, we consider annuities. An annuity is a sequence of payments, usually equal, made at equal intervals of time. An example of an annuity is a home mortgage which is paid monthly. The focus of this chapter is on simple annuities. A simple annuity is an annuity for which the payment interval and interest conversion period coincide, that is, the interest is compounded at the same frequency as the payments are made. An accumulated value or a future value of an annuity is the equivalent dated value of all payments due, at the end of the term. A discounted value or a present value of an annuity is the equivalent dated value of all payments due, at the beginning of the term. We derive the formulas for accumulated and discounted value of both ordinary annuity and annuity due, where ordinary annuity is an annuity for which periodic payments are made at the ends of payment intervals, while annuity due is an annuity for which periodic payments are made at the beginning of payment intervals. 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Payment interval · Term of an annuity · Periodic payment · Annuity certain · Contingent annuity · Perpetuity · Ordinary annuity · Annuity due · Deferred annuity · Simple annuity · General annuity · Accumulated value of an annuity · Discounted value of an annuity · Accumulation factor for payments at an interest rate · Discounting factor for payments at an interest rate 

An **annuity** is a sequence of payments, usually equal, made at equal intervals of time. The time between two successive payments of an annuity is called the **payment interval** . The time from the beginning of the first payment interval till the end of the last payment interval is called the **term** of an annuity. The payment at the beginning or at the end of each interval is called the **periodic payment** or the 

> © The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026 Z. Lukaˇc, _Economic Analysis Through Mathematics_ , Springer Texts in Business and Economics, https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-08812-3_26 

587 

26 Simple Annuities 

588 

**periodic rent** . An example of an annuity is a home mortgage which is paid monthly. Suppose that one takes a home mortgage of $100 000 for 20 years at 12 5% The term of this annuity is 20, the payment interval is 1 month, and the mortgage is repaid by monthly periodic payments. 

An **annuity certain** is an annuity for which the dates of the first and the last payment are fixed. An example of annuity certain is a home mortgage. A **contingent annuity** is an annuity for which the date of the first or the last payment (i.e., the term of an annuity) depends on some uncertain event. Life insurance premiums or payments from a retirement fund are typical examples of such annuities, because the beginning of payments depends on the uncertain event of a death of a person insured or the start of retirement. In this textbook, we will only deal with annuities certain. A **perpetuity** is an annuity with a defined starting date, but an infinite number of payments. 

Periodic payments can be made either at the beginning or at the end of each payment interval. An **ordinary annuity** is an annuity for which periodic payments are made at the ends of payment intervals (Fig. 26.1). 

An **annuity due** is an annuity for which periodic payments are made at the beginning of payment intervals (Fig. 26.2). A **deferred annuity** is an annuity whose first payment is due at some later date. 

A **simple annuity** is an annuity for which the payment interval and interest conversion period coincide, that is, the interest is compounded at the same frequency as the payments are made. A **general annuity** is an annuity for which the payment intervals are different from interest conversion periods. 

An **accumulated value** or a **future value** of an annuity is the equivalent dated value of all payments due, at the end of the term. A **discounted value** or a **present**

**value** of an annuity is the equivalent dated value of all payments due, at the beginning of the term. 

Here we will only consider simple annuities. 

We use the following notation: 

- : periodic payment of an annuity 

- : number of interest conversion periods during the term of an annuity (for simple annuities, this is also the total number of payments) 

- : interest rate per conversion period 

- : accumulated value, or the amount, of an annuity 

- : discounted value, or the present value, of an annuity 

Let us first derive the formula for the accumulated value of an ordinary simple annuity. Figure 26.3 shows the corresponding time diagram. 

The periodic payments of an ordinary annuity are made at the end of each payment interval. Therefore, the first payment is at the end of the first payment interval, one period after the starting date of the term of an annuity. The last payment is made at the end of the last payment interval. Note that the term of an annuity ends with that payment. 2. Derive the formula for the discounted value of a simple ordinary annuity. Use the time diagram and explain the notation used. 

3. Derive the formula for the accumulated value of a simple annuity due. Use the time diagram and explain the notation used. 

4. Derive the formula for the discounted value of a simple annuity due. Use the time diagram and explain the notation used.
