## ~~**Abstract**~~ 

One of the most important applications of annuities in business practice is repaying debts. Here, we will only consider the amortization method, which is also the most common method for repaying debts. In this method, the borrower repays the lender by a series of (usually equal) installment payments at periodic intervals. This process is called amortization of the loan. If all payments are equal and all payment intervals are equal, then payments form an annuity, and their discounted value is equal to the amount of the loan. Each payment pays the interest on the unpaid debt, and then the rest is used to repay a part of the remaining debt, thus reducing the outstanding debt and the interest paid in the following period. We start by showing how to compute the outstanding balance, that is, the outstanding debt at any given time. There are two methods on how to compute the outstanding balance: prospective method and retrospective method. Prospective method calculates the outstanding loan balance by looking into the future, that is, by finding the discounted value at that date of all the remaining installment payments. It can be applied if all the remaining payments, including the last concluding payment, are of equal size. However, that is very often not the case due to rounding, meaning that the last concluding installment payment is somewhat smaller than the previous ones, so the method cannot be applied. The second approach is the retrospective method. It calculates the outstanding loan balance looking into the past, that is, by writing the equation of value at the required date. The outstanding loan balance is calculated as the original amount of the loan accumulated to that date less the accumulated value (to that date) of all installment payments previously made. 

An amortization schedule is a table which shows the division of each payment into the principal and the interest part, together with the outstanding loan balance after each payment has been made. Here we show two models for an amortization schedule—the first one for level payments (the rate of interest is fixed over the term of the loan, the annuity payment periods coincide with the interest

605 

27 Amortization Schedules 

606 

conversion periods, and all annuity payments are level) and the second one for the case of equal installments of principal (periodic payments are not level, and the amount of principal repaid in each payment remains the same). 

## ~~**Keywords**~~ 

Amortization schedule · Periodic payment · Interest portion · Principal repaid portion · Outstanding balance · Models · Level payments · Equal installments of principal 

One of the most important application of annuities in business practice is repaying debts. Here, we will only consider the amortization method, which is also the most common method for repaying debts. In this method, the borrower repays the lender by a series of (usually equal) installment payments at periodic intervals. This process is called **amortization** of the loan. If all payments are equal and all payment intervals are equal, then payments form an annuity, and their discounted value is equal to the amount of the loan. Each payment pays the interest on the unpaid debt, and then the rest is used to repay a part of the remaining debt, thus reducing the outstanding debt and the interest paid in the following period. The outstanding debt at any time is called the **outstanding balance** or the **outstanding principal** . It can be obtained as the discounted value of all unmade payments. As time goes by, the outstanding balance decreases, and so does the interest portion of each payment, while the portion of the payment used to repay the debt increases. 

## **27.1 Outstanding Balance** 

For each periodic payment, we want to know how much is used to pay the interest on the outstanding balance and how much is used to repay part of the loan. Also, at any point of time, we want to know the amount of the outstanding balance. 

We use the following notation: 

: size of the loan : interest rate per conversion period : number of payments 

: outstanding loan balance at time (at the end of period : payment installment at time : amount of interest paid in the th installment : amount of principal repaid in the th installment : total interest paid over the life of a loan 

We assume that the rate of interest is fixed, that the annuity payment periods coincide with the interest conversion periods, and that all annuity payments are level, that is, for all 1

It remains to see how to find the outstanding loan balance. Obviously, 0 There are two approaches to the problem of determining the outstanding balance , that is, the outstanding balance after the th payment has been made. 

The first approach is the **prospective method** . It calculates the outstanding loan balance looking into the future, that is, by finding the discounted value at that date of all the remaining installment payments. It can be applied if all the remaining payments, including the last concluding payment, are of equal size. However, usually the installment payments are rounded up to the next cent or up to the next dime, meaning that the last concluding installment payment is somewhat smaller than the previous ones, so the method cannot be applied. ## **27.2 Amortization Schedule** 

An **amortization schedule** is a table which shows the division of each payment into the principal and the interest part, together with the outstanding loan balance after each payment has been made. As each periodic payment pays the interest on the unpaid balance and also repays a part of the outstanding principal, over the term of the loan the outstanding principal decreases, the interest portion of each payment decreases and the principal repaid portion increases.

## **27.4 Practice Problems** 

1. A loan of $8,000 is to be amortized with equal quarterly payments over 3 years at 4 15% Find the quarterly installments rounded up to the nearest dime, find the outstanding principal after 9 months (i.e., after 3 quarters), and split the 4th payment into principal and interest portion. Solution: 840, 3 6 318 18, 4 236 93, 4 603 17 

2. A loan of $12,000 is to be amortized with equal quarterly payments over 5 years at 12 9 5% Find the monthly installments rounded up to the nearest dime, find the outstanding principal after 18 months (i.e., 3 semiannual periods), and split the 4th payment into the principal and interest portion. Solution: 487 44, 3 3 885 28, 4 97 13, 4 390 3 

3. A loan of $5,000 is to be amortized with equal semiannual payments over 6 years at 2 5% Find the monthly installments rounded up to the nearest dime, find the outstanding principal after 21 months, and split the 22th payment into principal and interest portions. Solution: 252 1, 21 8 426 15, 22 66 71, 22 185 39 

4. Determine the monthly repayments needed to repay a $100,000 loan which is paid back over 30 years when the interest rate is 12 6% Solution: 599 55 

5. A loan is being repaid with 8 payments of $500 followed by 12 payments of $300 at the end of each month. If the nominal rate of interest convertible monthly is 6%, find the outstanding loan balance immediately after 5 payments have been made using (a) the prospective method and (b) the retrospective method. 

   - Solution: 4 919 04, 7 260 82 

6. Construct a complete amortization schedule for a $3,000 loan repaid in 5 annual payments if the annual effective rate of interest is 9%
